Text is never added this way, only a line's removal. | **Fixed** in c69d97b (fourth round, below). A removal names the edits that made it (`NoteLine.removedBy`, a set: concurrent removals are all kept), each an attributed value judged by its bytes like any other (`ItemMerge.attributedValues`), and a removal that names no writer is read as the work of the row carrying it, so a late one is late wherever it is published again. Tests: `lateNoteLineRemoval`, `lateUnstampedNoteLineRemoval` (suite "shared vaults through the session", `AdversarialSharedVaultLateWriteTests.swift`; both fail on the code before the fix), `removalsNameTheirWriter`, `unnamedPartsAreTheRowsOwn` ("Item merge: writers of removals, discards and history"), `LongRunningLateWriteSimulationTests`. |
| S33 | Low | Device IDs are chosen by devices, so an insider (A4) can list another member's device ID in their own account's registry. Segments are attributed by signature, which only one account's keys make; but a value stamped with that device ID that this device never saw in the device's own stream counts only while every listing account's work counts, so once the insider leaves, the other member's values that reached this device only inside others' versions stop counting here. | **Accepted**: denial of service by an insider, as S11, and visible as held-aside work. Since S36 such a value counts only through the segment that carried its bytes, so this is left only where S42's vouching applies. The audit of 2026-11-17 found "attributed by signature" too weak: a segment the insider signs into the stream named after that device ID was merged as that device's versions; see Z46. |
| S34 | Info | A member invited after a rotation holds no earlier key. Segments sealed under a key the chain never entitled them to are passed over (they never open), and what they carried reaches the member in the versions rotation sealed again; a value of a departed account in such a version counts because a key introduced at or after the record that ended that account's permission opens only for someone who holds the record. | **By design** (§8.5, §8.6); a record nobody with the old key sealed again stays unreadable to the newcomer. |
| S35 | Low | The ledger, the pins, the stream index and the plans are per device. Another device of a member's account receives the vault's keys through the account's own vault directory, but until it accepts the invitation itself it holds no ledger and judges the vault as one of its own account's, refusing other members' objects. | **Tracked** for milestone 4's open list: syncing a member's sharing state to their own other devices (signed by the account, like the vault directory). The audit of 2026-11-17 found this row understated: such a device also wrote, sealed under the lowest key ID, maintained the log and could not accept the invitation; see Z41, which closes the vault there. The syncing stays tracked. |

**Integration review.** Two independent reviews of the integration above, in a remote Linux session; S36, S38 and S40
were reproduced against it with temporary tests before anything was changed, S37, S39 and S41 confirmed by reading the
code and then reproduced by the regression tests, which fail on the reviewed code (S37 by a late rename, S41 by a
lock between recording a segment and merging it). None was rejected. S42 and S43 are what the fixes leave. Tests are
in the suite "Adversarial: shared vaults through the session" (`AdversarialSharedVaultAttributionTests.swift`).

| ID | Severity | Finding and threat | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| S36 | High | S28 was still open. The judge took a value's author from the revision in its own `GroupStamp`, conflict, retained value or note line, and the stream index only proved that the named device's stream carried *some* version of that number. Both come from the writer's plaintext. A former member (A5) whose client still writes, with a store (A1) withholding the removal from a remaining member's device, could relabel a late value with a revision a remaining member really wrote (or back-date it to their own in-cut one, or name a version never published): once the unaware device merged it and wrote on top, the value counted on every device, the owner's and a member invited later included. | **Fixed.** A value is judged by its *bytes*, stamp included (`ItemMerge.attributedValues`, SHA-256 under `WW-value-v1`): the index files, for each segment, the values it carried whose stamp names its own stream's device (`SharedStreamIndex` format 2), and a value counts only through a segment of that device's stream that carried those exact bytes and counts itself (inside the cut, for an ended account). A value naming *this* device that none of its segments carried counts only if its own copy holds it, so the device named by a relabelled value refuses it at once. A value carried nowhere here waits for its device's stream (`awaiting`, which asks for a replay when a new segment of it arrives) unless S42's vouching applies; the old "every listing account may write now" rule is gone. Tests: `relabelledAsARemainingMember`, `backDatedToAnInCutRevision`. |
| S37 | Medium | The same root cause for the vault's description, judged by the row's origin only: `VaultMetadataMerger` stores a remote description at an equal version again as this device's own row, so a former member's late rename, merged that way by an unaware member, became that member's work everywhere. | **Fixed.** `VaultMetadata.writtenBy` records the write that chose the description whenever a row is sealed again (the merger, rotation's re-seal, a password change); the judge attributes the description to it, by its bytes, as for items. Test: `lateRenameStoredAgain`. |
| S38 | Medium | Key IDs were chosen locally and are public (grant names, envelope headers); with sharing, the owner of a vault one is invited to chooses them. `adoptVaultKey` skipped the wrap row when one existed for another vault and then replaced the resident key under that ID, and `fetchCurrentKey` took any resident key with the ID as this vault's. A hostile owner (A4) giving their vault's key the ID of a key the invitee holds made the invitee's vault unreadable until a relaunch and left the new vault awaiting its key for good. | **Fixed.** A key whose ID this device holds, resident or stored, for another vault is refused (`SharedVaultError.keyIDInUse`) in `acceptInvitation` before anything is held and in `adoptVaultKey`; `fetchCurrentKey` asks for a key held *for this vault*. Test: `keyIDOfAnotherVault`. |
| S39 | Medium | Other members' registries were anchored on the first signing key met while iterating every shared vault, and stored per account. An administrator of one vault (A4 there) who lists another account's ID with keys of their own, and publishes a registry under that ID, could have their device verify as that account in a second vault where it may write, depending on dictionary order, so devices also disagreed. | **Fixed.** Registries are evaluated per (account, signing key) and each vault verifies, lists devices and retires authors through the anchors *its* chain gives (`memberRegistries`); an object signed by a member whose chain keys differ from this device's pin (`MemberKeyCheck.substituted`) is refused. Test: `registriesAnchoredPerVault`. |
| S40 | High | Erasures escaped the cut. A former member's late permanent deletion, merged by a device before it knew of the removal, was remembered in the engine's erasures, which a reconciling replay keeps "so that an erased record stays erased": once shown the removal the device held the deletion's segment aside and still kept the item erased, while the owner's device kept it. Data loss by someone whose work does not count (F7.6, F7.7), and divergence. | **Fixed.** A vault with a judge (whose log is whole: no snapshots, no collection) rebuilds its erasures from the log when it replays, so only erasures from segments that count are applied again. Test: `lateErasureOfARemovedMember`. |
| S41 | Low | Receiving a key, or a segment inside a cut, asked for a replay in memory only. A restart before the replay ran (a lock, the app killed, the transport gone) left versions held aside for good: the saved head was unchanged, so nothing replayed. | **Fixed.** A replay is asked for by clearing the saved index's `judgedHead` as well (`requestReplay`), and the index is written before anything a recorded segment lets count is merged; a reconciling run that starts is persistent by itself (its reset state is saved). A segment is no longer recorded while locked (it is recorded once, so it must be with its values), and an index refused on load is rebuilt by a replay before anything is taken out against it. Test: `replayOutlastsARestart`. |
| S42 | Low | What S36 leaves. A value no segment held here carried is vouched for only on a device that passes over segments (its account joined after a rotation, so it cannot open what carried older values), and only in a version sealed under a key introduced at or after the latest record that ended anyone's permission to write; elsewhere it waits for its stream. So a member invited after a *demotion*, with no rotation since, holds aside versions carrying values from before the last rotation until the next one seals them again; and a store (A1) that withholds one device's stream from another delays every version carrying that device's values there. | **Accepted**: the vouching rule is what keeps a late write sealed under an old key, by a former member's choice, from reaching a newcomer through a device that merged it before it knew. Rotating after a demotion, before inviting, avoids the first; the second is withholding, as everywhere, and visible as held-aside work. |
| S43 | Low | Two parts of an item name no writer: `history` (a set that only grows, so a late value there destroys nothing but can add entries) and `discards` (what a resolution had before it). A former member's late version whose discards cover retained values, merged by an unaware device, removes those retained values from its copy and from what it publishes. | **Fixed** in c69d97b (fourth round, below). Each history entry names the writes that recorded it (`ItemHistoryEntry.writtenBy`, set by `ItemMerge.stamped`, never by the caller) and each discard the resolution that made it (`ItemDiscard`); both are attributed values, and an unnamed one is the row's own. A late discard or entry counts on no device, and a device that merged one takes out every version holding it and replays, so the retained values are back and the entry is in no history; no entry of a version that counts is ever removed. Tests: `lateDiscardOfRetainedValues` (the discard back-dated to an in-cut revision), `lateHistoryEntry` (the entry naming no writer; both fail on the code before the fix), `historyEntriesNameEveryWriter`, `discardsNameTheirWriter`, `legacyDiscardsDecode`, `LongRunningLateWriteSimulationTests`. |

**Fourth round.** S32 and S43 were fixed at the owner's direction (c69d97b), in a remote Linux session (swift-crypto, not
CryptoKit): every part of an item now names the write that made it (`10-sync-correctness.md` §9 gives the argument
that the judge, so extended, catches every version holding a late write's work and that devices converge whenever
they learnt of the cut). Each regression test was first run against the code before the fix and failed there. The
randomised simulation written for the fix found S44, reproduced through the session before it was fixed; S45 and S46
were found by reading what S44 left and have not been reproduced.

| ID | Severity | Finding and threat | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| S44 | Medium | A late write's *knowledge* escaped the cut. A device that merged a late version and edited the same group wrote a value whose stamp had seen everything the late writer had, and the late value, shown and replaced, was not retained: the new version held nothing attributed to the late write, counted everywhere, and superseded honest values the device had been shown only through it. A demoted member (A4 until then) whose client has not been shown the record, a store (A1) withholding it and, for a while, the owner's stream from another member's device: the owner's rename, merged by the demoted member's client before its late title, was lost on every device once the member renamed the item again (titles have no history). | **Fixed.** The judge also checks every edit a version's clock says it contains (`knowledgeCounts`, which covers every stamp's `seen`): for a device whose account's work does not all count, a segment of its stream that counts must have carried a version of the record at or above that count, or the version is held aside (waiting, if that stream is not here; vouched for as values are, S42). A version built on a late write is held aside like the write. Test: `lateKnowledgeOfADemotedMember` (fails without the check). |
| S45 | Medium | A permanent deletion's erasure summary is the deleting device's clock. A device that merged a late version and then deleted the item permanently claims, in its tombstone, what the late writer had seen, and erases on every other device honest values it was shown only through the late write. Reproduced (fifth round, §O): the owner renames an item just before removing a member, whose client merges the rename and writes a late title; the remaining member's device, not shown the removal, merges both at once (the late title superseding the rename), and the person trashes and deletes the item; the owner's device erased it, rename and all. Data loss by someone whose work does not count (F7.6, F7.7). Raised from Low once reproduced. | **Fixed** (§O). `WriteJudge.admitsErasure`: before a tombstone is applied or remembered, every element of its summary is judged by `knowledgeCounts` as a version's clock is; one that fails is held aside like a version (the item stays, the deletion kept for the person), and this device's own published deletion that fails asks for a replay, which puts back what it erased. Test: `deletionOnTopOfALateWrite` (fails without the check). |
| S46 | Medium | A former member's late version can claim, in its vectors, to have seen edits of a *current* member that do not exist yet. A device that merges it carries the claim in its clock, which the S44 check took on the current member's word, as every current member's claims were (`10-sync-correctness.md` §6); an edit made on that copy then supersedes those edits when they are made, if their writer had seen nothing the device had not. Reproduced (§O): a late version changing no value and claiming the owner's edits up to a version the owner had not reached, merged by an unaware member whose rename on top of it then dropped the owner's concurrent rename on every device. Raised from Low once reproduced. | **Fixed** (§O). `knowledgeCounts` checks every listed device in the clock but the version's own writer: a current member's count too must be backed by a segment of their stream that carried a version of the record at or above it; until it is, the version waits for that stream (`awaiting`), and a claim about the judging device's own edits beyond what it published is refused. So an honest device merges a claim only once it has come true. What is left is S48. Test: `lateClaimOfFutureEdits` (fails without the check); `lateKnowledgeOfADemotedMember` (S44) now shows the owner's stream to the other member's device, which before would not merge the late title at all. |

Accepted from the second round, beside S2 and S12 as amended: a former administrator who can still write to the store can
publish valid records on dead branches without limit; a device keeps 1,024 and pays a signature check per sync for
each one beyond (§8.8). A fork between honest administrators costs work: what lost is done again, removals by any
device that may and everything else by its author's.

What the review did not find, having looked: a parser that reads past its input or allocates on a count's say-so;
an identity taken from a record rather than from where it was found (vault, key and recipient of a grant, the vault
of a chain, the account of a directory answer); a path from an unverified bundle to a wrapped key; a signature under
`SignatureDomain.membership` that verifies as another kind of message; key material leaving `SecureBytes` (the
planner's new key exists only inside HPKE grants and is zeroised when planning returns).

## J. Milestone 3 review (multiple vaults, enrolment, device registry)

Review date: 2026-11-15. Written and reproduced in a remote Linux session against swift-crypto (ADR 0006); nothing
below has run on a Mac yet. The design was attacked while it was built: W1 and W2 came from the adversarial suite
"account enrolment and device registry", W3 from the first run of the real-enrolment tests, the rest from reading
the design against the adversaries of architecture §2 plus two that this work introduces: **a revoked device that
kept the keyset and its own device key**, and **an insider device in hands that do not know the account password**.

| ID | Severity | Finding and threat | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| W1 | High | A device that had not yet been given a new password replayed the password change among the entries no checkpoint had sealed. A revoked device holding the account key could sign a *back-dated* revocation of the device that made the change; where its entry ID sorted first, the change was void on that device, which then could never follow it, kept the revoked device, and dropped the one that revoked it. | **Fixed.** A candidate password change is judged by replaying the registry with it as the last checkpoint, never by its place among unsealed entries. Tests: `pendingChangeSurvivesBackdating` (over seeds, asserting the bad order occurs), `revokedDeviceCannotReturn`. |
| W2 | Medium | Entries a device wrote while it was being revoked could be replayed before the revocation (the order among concurrent entries was "revocations first among those ready", and a revocation may become ready late), so a device it added at that moment stayed registered. | **Fixed.** Each revocation is replayed as early as causality allows: what it had seen, the revocation, then everything else. Test: `deterministic`. |
| W3 | Low | Two devices given the same signing key (here: two test devices built from one seed) were both registered. A key that serves two devices makes "which device wrote this" meaningless and lets a revoked key return under another ID. | **Fixed.** No key serves two devices, and a revoked key is refused under any ID. Tests: `keysAreFixed`, `revocationIsFinal`. |
| W4 | Medium | A device whose own header was out of date (the password had been changed elsewhere), or that had been revoked, put its old header back on the transport when it found it missing, undoing the changing device's removal of it. | **Fixed.** A device republishes its header only if it follows the current credentials and is registered. An attacker with store access can still replay an old header; that is `replayedHeader`. |
| W5 | Medium | Enrolment tried the three newest headers by name, so three planted objects under newer names used up the budget without one derivation being worth making. | **Fixed.** Only a header that parses, matches its name and has in-bounds KDF parameters costs a derivation; up to 64 names are looked at. A store that plants three *well-formed* headers still exhausts the budget — it can as easily delete the real one (Y7). Test: `plantedHeaders`. |
| W6 | Medium | Creating the registry for an account that had none (one made before this work) at unlock would have let a database restored without its registry rows start a second registry for an account that already syncs, splitting it. | **Fixed.** The registry is started for such an account only at a sync that finds no account header on the transport. Otherwise the device reports `deviceRegistryUnavailable` and must enrol again. Test: `legacyAccount`. |
| W7 | Medium | Design: what a password change on one device means for the others. Left per-device, a password retired because someone learned it would keep opening every other device. | **Decided.** The change is the account's: a credential entry signed with the previous generation's enrolment key, a new header, and every other device refusing the old password once it holds that header. It needs the current password, so a device in the wrong hands cannot lock the person out (`passwordChangeNeedsThePassword`); if someone who does know it changes it, the person takes the account back from a device they hold (`hostilePasswordChange`). |
| W8 | Medium | Limit, stated rather than fixed: revocation stops writing, not reading (D9b above); a revocation that keeps the password is sound only if the device's holder never had the keyset; and whatever a revoked device wrote before a device learned of the revocation is merged there and handed on. | **Accepted and documented** in architecture §7.8, with the API making the password choice explicit and a test that pins the limit (`revocationKeepingThePassword`). The app must default to changing the password. |
| W9 | Low | A snapshot whose coverage a device had already merged was skipped. After a revocation a device's snapshot may be the only carrier of what it merged from the revoked device, including rows that device attributed to others (Y15). | **Changed.** Snapshot entries are always merged (merging is idempotent). Residual, accepted: rows a revoked insider *fabricated* in a snapshot, merged by one device before it learned of the revocation, reach devices that sync while that device's hand-on snapshot is listed; a device offline for that whole time may never get those fabricated rows. No value a person typed is affected. |
| W10 | Low | A deleted vault's objects stay on the transport, and its key in every device's store, indefinitely: late arrivals must stay readable so that an unseen edit is kept. | **Accepted** for now; collecting them needs every registered device's acknowledgement of the deletion. Tracked in architecture §16. |
| W11 | Info | The software device key is in the Keychain, not hardware-bound: a thief who can read the Keychain has it. It is one of two signatures; the account key still needs the password. | **Tracked**: the Secure Enclave adapter (P-256; verification, with the low-S rule, is built and has a known answer from Python `cryptography`). The Secret Key is in the same position, and the documents said otherwise until Z107. |
| W12 | High | An account-key holder — a registered insider, or a revoked device that kept the keyset — can fill the registry to its 4,096-entry limit, after which every device refuses to evaluate it and sync stops. Reproduced for a revoked device after a `.change` revocation: entries it signed counted for nothing but were admitted, stored and counted, so the revoking device's sync broke for good, later revocations stopped spreading, and enrolment failed (review "flood", W20); and again, after that fix, with entries in the names of puppet devices it registered itself (W26). | **Fixed for a revoked device once a password change seals its revocation**, in its own name and in its puppets' (W20, W26). **Accepted** for a registered insider, and for a revoked device before a password change: what they sign may count, and the limit refuses the registry rather than evaluate it without bound (SEC9); the same party can delete every object in the store. |
| W13 | High | Found by the vault simulation (`LongRunningVaultSimulationTests`): a deletion made on a device was dropped as "already published" whenever that device knew *any* erasure of the same record with a later stamp, even another device's. That one's author may have seen less — its erasure kept a version this deletion had seen — so the record was erased on the deleting device and kept on every other: divergence, and a deletion the person made that never happened elsewhere. In the engine since milestone 3; moves between vaults (trash, then delete) made it frequent. | **Fixed.** A deletion is done only when *this device* has published it. Erasures of one record are remembered as their join (`RecordMerger.joinErasureSummaries`), and every version is judged against the join, so which of two erasures a device learns of first decides nothing. Tests: `deletionAfterAnotherDevicesErasure`, and the simulation, which failed on roughly one seed in ten before; the one further failure it found afterwards was W18. |
| W14 | Medium | Found by the same simulation: what a vault's deletion left behind was *written* into the trash by each device (a version of its own per device). The next deletion or move of that item, made on one device, had not seen the other devices' versions and so had to keep them: the item kept coming back, kept after deletion, on devices that had done nothing. | **Fixed.** What a deleted vault still holds is *shown* in the trash, as kept after a deletion, and never written that way: derived from the signed directory, the same on every device. |
| W15 | Medium | A header from before a password change, put back in the local file (A8) *together with* deleting the registry's entries from it, unlocked with the retired password: the rollback check needed the registry to tell which credentials the device followed. | **Fixed.** The Keychain anchor records the credential generation, and the keyset's own wrap is bound to the generation it was made for; the check needs nothing else from the file and runs before any key is installed. Test: `localHeaderRollback`, three variants. |
| W16 | Low | With `WATCHWORD_TEST_SEED` set, every `TestVault` took the same seed, so all of an account's test devices had one device key, which the registry refuses (W3): every enrolment in the nightly run would have failed. | **Fixed** in the test support: the nightly seed is added to each vault's own. |
| W17 | Medium | Two smaller faults in the same code, found while fixing W13. (a) A version kept after an erasure was written two above the highest *edit* the erasure's summary named, but had to be above the erasure's *stamp*, which follows the record's version and climbs with every merge; when it was not, the engine refused the merger's result and the object carrying it was rejected at every sync. (b) An erasure a device already held, met again in a later snapshot, was applied to the local version again, putting an item the person had restored back in the trash. | **Fixed.** The engine passes the version to write above (`resolveErasure(_:of:above:)`), and an erasure that adds nothing to what the device holds is not applied again except by a reconciling sync. Tests: `deletionAfterAnotherDevicesErasure`, `restoredItemStaysRestored`. |
| W18 | High | Found by the vault simulation after W13 was fixed: the person deletes a version that this device's own earlier erasure had kept (a trash made elsewhere that the erasure had not seen). A kept version keeps its own number, so the second deletion's stamp was lower than the first, and the outbox dropped it as "already published by this device": erased here, kept on every other device. | **Fixed.** A pending deletion counts as published only if this device published a deletion at or after its stamp *and* the join of the erasures it knows already holds everything this one had seen. Test: `secondDeletionBySameDevice` (fails without the fix); the simulation seed that found it (`WATCHWORD_TEST_SEED=77`) passes, as do 20 seeds of the standard run and 3 of the long one (Linux). |
| W19 | Low | A8 edits a vault-key wrap row in the local file (moves it under another vault, corrupts it). Nothing opened under the wrong key — the wrap's context names its vault — but the vault stayed unreadable for good: the sync fetched only wraps whose row was *missing*. | **Fixed.** While unlocked, a row that does not open counts as missing, and the next sync replaces it (one version up) with the transport's copy, which is checked against its name, its signatures and the keyset first. Test: `localVaultKeyWraps`. |
| W20 | High | The registry flood (W12). Admission checked only the account key's signature, so entries nobody could ever apply — by a device revoked behind a checkpoint, in another device's name, with a superseded enrolment key, with no parents — were stored by every device and counted against the limit, and a sync fetched at most 4,096 names in sorted order. | **Fixed.** `DeviceRegistry.evaluate` names what can never count and retains everything else (`retainedEntries`); devices store, publish and count only that, prune the rest, and fetch in batches judged with what they hold, skipping for the session what they found worthless. A device's entry must descend from its own registration, and that registration must itself count (W26). Test: `registryFlood` (4,096 planted entries of three kinds; fails without the fix in all three ways), `retention`, `sizeLimit`, `evaluationLimit`. |
| W21 | High | Found while fixing W20: an entry appended after a checkpoint could reorder the layer the checkpoint sealed — a revocation pulled forward everything it had seen, in any layer — so a revoked device could move one of its back-dated, sealed entries (a device it added) in front of its own revocation, after the password change. | **Fixed.** A revocation moves only entries of its own layer, and one that can never count moves nothing. Test: `sealedOrder` (over seeds, asserting the bad order occurs; fails without the fix). |
| W22 | High | A `lock()` landing while the account's half of a sync awaited a vault-key fetch, or while the reconciliation after an unlock awaited the store or the Keychain, left key material read after it was zeroised: a trap, so a crash, from an auto-lock. | **Fixed.** Both re-check before touching keys; a lock ends the sync's account half as a download, and the reconciliation quietly. Tests: suite "Vault session: a lock during the account's objects". |
| W23 | Medium | A move publishes its copy and its original's deletion in two vaults' logs, by two engine runs; a sync that wrote the source log and not the destination's removed the item from every other device. | **Fixed.** `syncAll` publishes deletions last, only once every vault's push in that sync succeeded (`SyncEngine.sync(deferringDeletions:)`). Tests: `interruptedMovePublication`, `deferredDeletion`. |
| W24 | Medium | A store restored onto other hardware registers as a new device, but the edits and deletions its old device had not synced still carried the old device's ID, and the engine publishes only its own rows: they reached nobody. | **Fixed.** Registration carries them over to the new ID (rows written again unchanged, pending deletions re-recorded), and each vault's sync state starts over. Test: `restoredStoreCarriesUnsyncedWork`. |
| W25 | Low | A vault deletion's local row kept its author's signature and was judged against the current registry, so revoking the device that deleted a vault brought the vault back on devices that had verified the deletion, until another device's copy arrived. | **Fixed.** A device that has verified a deletion and can sign makes the local row its own (`adoptVerifiedDeletions`). A deletion seen only while locked still rests on its author until then. Test: `deletionOutlivesItsAuthor`. |
| W26 | High | W20's fix could be bypassed with puppets. An entry by a device counted as "might count" if *any* ancestor registered that device under a key that verifies it, whether or not that registration could itself count, and nothing void made its descendants void. So the revoked device, after a `.change` revocation, planted an `add` of a device whose key it made (with a garbage author signature, or signed by its own revoked key, or as an enrolment under a password change of its own) and then any number of entries in the puppet's name: all verified against the void registration, the puppet was never revoked, and all were retained, along with the registration through what they descend from. 100 such entries were 106 retained; 4,096 made every device refuse the registry, so a later revocation's password change never spread and a new device could not enrol. A puppet registering a second puppet, validly signed, went one step further. | **Fixed.** Voidness propagates, in one causal-order pass per replay: past the last checkpoint a device's entry is void unless it descends from a registration of its author, under a key that verifies it, that is not void, and an enrolment is void if the credential entry it cites is; a sealed registration may be relied on only if it counted. The replay applies the same rule (a device's entry needs a registration that counted), so nothing void is ever valid. Each distinct registered key is tried once per entry when the graph is built, not once per replay. Tests: `puppets` (a garbage-signed registration, one signed by the revoked key, one a checkpoint sealed as void, a chain of two puppets, an enrolment under the revoked device's own password change; 64 entries each and one at 4,096; fails without the fix), `puppetFlood` (4,096 entries in three puppets' names at session level; fails without the fix: the next password change never reaches B). |
| W27 | High | Found while fixing W26: the cost of evaluating the registry was quadratic in what a key holder plants. An entry's author signature was tried against every distinct key its ancestors registered its author under, so about 2,000 registrations of one device ID under distinct keys, chained, and 2,000 entries in that ID's name descending from all of them (well within `maximumEvaluated`) were 4,196,360 signature verifications: every device's evaluation hung (31 minutes for the test below, in a debug build on Linux). | **Fixed.** An entry whose ancestors register its author under more than `maximumKeysPerDevice` (2) distinct keys is void without any signature being checked; otherwise each of its at most two keys is tried once, when the graph is built, and replays read the result (an enrolment's signature, too, is checked once there rather than in every replay). Verifications are at most two per entry plus one per credential link replayed. The rule reads ancestry only, so it does not depend on arrival order; honest histories register an ID under one key. Test: `manyKeysForOneDevice` (2,048 registrations and 2,048 entries: 2,056 verifications for 4,102 entries, asserted against a bound of 2 per entry; with the bound removed, 4,196,360 verifications and 1,877 seconds, and the bound fails). |

Also recorded: `UITestPeer` (in `Apps/`, not touched by this work) still enrols its second session by copying the
account header and vault-key wraps, which no longer makes a registered device; it must use `VaultSession.enrol`.
`SystemKeychainStore` gained the two new Keychain item names without being compiled; the "Keychain item protection"
suite now iterates `KeychainItem.all`, also uncompiled here.

## K. Owner decisions (2026-11-16)

Decisions the owner made on open questions and review items, each carried out in its own commit with tests and the
documents that describe the behaviour. The threat each one moves is named, and so is what it leaves.

| ID | Decision | Threat and residual | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| K1 | CodeQL's weak-hash alert on `PasswordDigest` (SHA-1) is not fixed. | None: the Pwned Passwords range API is defined over SHA-1 and only a five-character prefix leaves the device; the hash protects nothing (`02-architecture.md` §13.2). | **Accepted, won't fix.** Recorded in `07-supply-chain.md` §3.4 (alert dispositions) and at the call site. |
| K2 | CSV export writes exact values; nothing neutralises spreadsheet formulas, on export or import. The CSV is for another password manager's importer, not a spreadsheet. | Residual, accepted: a person who opens the exported file in a spreadsheet anyway may have a cell that an import (A7) or a vault member (A4) planted run as a formula. In exchange no password is changed by an apostrophe the other app keeps. The export screen's standing warning says the file holds every password in plain text and is not meant for spreadsheets (`InterchangeGuide`, `notForSpreadsheets`). | **Changed.** `CSVWriter.FormulaPolicy`, `neutralised`, `cellNeutralised` and `possiblyNeutralisedFormula` removed; `formulaInjection` now asserts byte-exact round trips (PRD SEC7). |
| K3 | AutoFill fills only `https` pages (PRD F6.9). | A2 on the network path serving a real site over `http`: the gate refuses (`FillRefusal.insecurePage`) every list, release (by choice too), save and generate request for an `http` URL. Two residuals the extension cannot see, stated in `02-architecture.md` §9 and `05-anti-phishing.md` §4: a form on an `https` page that submits to `http`, and a QuickType suggestion the system offers on an `http` page (a tap hands back the published identity, a domain, and `ServiceScope` records no scheme). The hygiene report flags saved `http://` addresses. | **Changed.** Tests: "Request model: pages loaded over plain http", `plainHTTPPage`, `plainHTTP`. |
| K4 | Ownership transfer of a shared vault is wanted, in a future version. | Nothing in the format, the rules or the fork rule precluded it, but a device on this version took a later-format record for junk and carried on, so a former owner's records would have split it from updated devices. | **Deferred, prepared.** `02-architecture.md` §8.9 is the design; devices on this version now stop the chain at an owner-signed later-format record (`continuesInLaterFormat`). Tests: `laterFormatStopsTheChain`, `laterFormatImpostors`. |
| K5 | The embedded EFF wordlist is verified against EFF's own file. | Supply chain (T3): the list came from a mirror. EFF's `eff_large_wordlist.txt`, downloaded 2026-11-16, has the mirror's SHA-256 and the same 7,776 words in order. | **Closed.** |
| K6 | An imported `www.<apex>` host also matches `<apex>`, by default; opt-out. | A widening, chosen: an item a file saved for `www.example.com` is offered on `example.com` (and its subdomains when the `www` scope included them). Bounded: one `www` label only, never a public suffix or an address, reported per entry, and never added to an existing item without the person's choice (`MergeChoices`). | **Changed.** `ImportOptions.matchApexOfWWWHosts` (default on) replaces the opt-in `stripsLeadingWWW`. The import screen offers the opt-out since Z68. |
| K7 | The passkey AAGUID stays `5B0C7A3E-9D41-4F6C-A2B8-3E1F7C0D9A54`. | None: it names the authenticator model, is not a secret and not per user; unregistered in the community AAGUID list as of 2026-11-16. | **Documented** in `README.md` and at `PasskeyAuthenticator.aaguid`. |
| K8 | Shadowed accounts are no longer listed in the AutoFill extension. | None new: a shadowed account was listed and never released; now it is neither, and stays reachable in the app (PRD F6.6). S1's retail login is neither listed nor released on the AWS console. | **Changed.** `CredentialList.otherAccounts` and `OtherAccountEntry` removed; `NewItemProposal.hiddenAccounts` kept. |

## L. Integration review (the kit stream: AutoFill and hygiene in the session)

Review date: 2026-11-16. Written and reproduced in a remote Linux session against swift-crypto (ADR 0006); nothing
below has run on a Mac yet. The review read the session's hygiene wiring (`VaultSession+Hygiene.swift`,
`DeviceLocalKeys`) against the adversaries of architecture §2 and §13.1, with one added: **another device of the
same account**, which holds the keyset (a revoked one keeps it, §7.8). Each finding was reproduced by a failing test
before it was fixed, and the test stays as the regression.

| ID | Severity | Finding and threat | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| L1 | Medium | The device-local key that seals the scan ledger and the prefix cache was `HKDF(Ksym, salt: accountID ‖ deviceID)`. Its only secret was Ksym, which every device of the account holds, and a device ID is on every row and stream the device writes. So another device of the account (a revoked one included), with A8's access to this device's files, derived this device's key and record names, opened its ledger (item IDs, versions, breach counts) and its cached prefixes (twenty bits of each password's SHA-1), and planted a ledger saying a breached password had been checked and found clear: the session believed it, and the warning stayed hidden until the next weekly pass. The docs and the adversarial suite claimed the opposite; the test behind the claim only compared two *different* device IDs. Reported twice by the review. | **Fixed.** The local key also takes a device secret: 32 random bytes made at this device's first unlock and kept only in its Keychain (`KeychainItem.deviceLocalSecret`, device-only, erased with the account), read in the engine's unlock task: `HKDF(Ksym ‖ secret, salt: accountID ‖ deviceID)`. A keyset holder claiming this device's ID derives another key; the secret alone opens nothing. Tests: `ledgerForgedByAKeysetHolderIsIgnored` (the adversary holds the keyset and this device's ID, reads the ledger and plants a forged one under both names; failed in all four expectations before the fix), `localKeyIsPerDevice` (now with a claimant of this device's ID), `deviceSecretIsInTheKeychain`. |
| L2 | Low | `lock()` zeroised the keyset and the local key but locked the engine in a task it did not wait for, so the reuse key, the reuse tokens and every analysis stayed resident, and the engine answered reuse queries, for as long as the session's current job kept the task out (`deleteAccount` after its `lock()`), or for as long as an iOS process stayed suspended after locking in the background: a memory image (A3, A8) found what the design says is gone at lock. The adversarial test waited for that task before it asserted anything. | **Fixed.** The engine runs on the session's serial executor (both conform to `SharesExecutor` over one `SerialExecutorOwner`, in `SecureMemory` because reading another actor's executor is an unsafe construct; ADR 0001, amendment of 2026-11-16), so `lock()` drops the engine's keys and everything derived from a password synchronously (`HygieneEngine.dropKeys()` through `assumeIsolated`) before it returns; only emptying the prefix cache is left to the task. Engine and session no longer run concurrently, so a lock can never race an engine computation on another thread. Test: `engineIsLockedWhenLockReturns` (twenty lock and unlock cycles, asking the engine before any queued task can run; failed in every cycle before the fix). |
| L3 | Low | Rollback of the ledger: it has a fixed name and record version, so an older sealed ledger of this device's own, kept and put back by A8, opens and is believed. | **Accepted.** An older ledger is an authentic earlier reading of this device: results are per item version, so it says nothing about a version written since, and every check it records is older than the one it replaces, as is the weekly pass it describes, so nothing is due later than it was; what can change is that the report shows the older reading until that check runs. Binding the ledger to a counter would need a Keychain write at every stored ledger, which a background scan while the device is locked cannot make (the Keychain items are `WhenPasscodeSetThisDeviceOnly`), and a ledger whose counter did not advance would then open as nothing: every unlock would ask for everything again, the burst the ledger's failure record exists to prevent. Test pinning the bound: `rolledBackLedgerDefersNothing`. |
| L4 | Low | `DeviceLocalKeys` was `public`: any app, extension or adapter code holding a `Keyset` could derive the hygiene reuse key or a device-local key, outside the session's lock-epoch checks. `InMemoryDeviceLocalStore.contents`, a test accessor, was public on a production type. | **Fixed.** Both are `package`, like `EncryptionKey.material`; the tests reach them across the package boundary. |

Also recorded: `SystemKeychainStore` gained the `device-local-secret` item name without being compiled here, and the
"Keychain item protection" suite, which iterates `KeychainItem.all`, covers it on a Mac only. The engine now shares
the session's executor, so the analysis an unlock makes runs between the session's own jobs rather than beside them
(one item at a time, as before); `HygieneEngine`'s public initialiser still gives an engine an executor of its own.

## M. Integration review (the interchange stream: import, export and the encrypted archive in the session)

Review date: 2026-11-16. Written and reproduced in a remote Linux session against swift-crypto (ADR 0006); nothing
below has run on a Mac yet. The review read `VaultSession+Interchange.swift`, `AccountArchive` and `ArchivedItems`
against the adversaries of architecture §2, chiefly **A7** (whoever hands the person an import file or archive) and
**A1** (the cloud drive an archive is kept in). Each finding but X2 was reproduced by a test that fails without the
fix (checked by reverting the fix), and the test stays as the regression.

| ID | Severity | Finding and threat | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| X1 | Low | `previewArchiveImport` ran the archive's PBKDF2 on the unlocked session actor, at the iteration count in the file's cleartext header, before anything in it was authenticated. The ceiling (25M) stops a hang but not a delay chosen by the file: about 4 s on this VM, longer on a phone, and every retry repeats it. Meanwhile `lock()`, auto-lock and sync could not run, and the vault keys stayed resident; "lock always wins" held only across awaits, and this path had none. | **Fixed.** The derivation, decryption and decoding run off the actor (`awayFromSession`), touching none of the session's keys; the epoch is checked when they return, so a lock takes effect at once and the preview throws `locked`. Not bounded against the session's own parameters: an archive made on a faster device, or by an earlier account, legitimately has more iterations than this device would calibrate. Tests: `lockWinsDuringArchiveDerivation` (a stand-in for the derivation blocks until the test has locked; with the work back on the actor the lock waits out the 60-second bound and the test fails), `lockWinsDuringArchivePreview` (the same through `previewArchiveImport` itself, held inside its derivation by the session's `awayWorkHook`, so the wiring is guarded and not only the helper), `raisedIterations`. |
| X2 | Low | The archive's creation time and identifier are authenticated (the manifest covers the header) but the preview dropped them, so a restore could not show which archive it was. A1 can replace the newest archive in the cloud drive with a year-old one under the same passphrase and Secret Key, and nothing on screen would differ. | **Fixed.** `ImportPreview.archiveID` and `archiveCreatedAt`, set only once the archive has opened; the restore screen must show the date (milestone 7, open). Test: `archiveDate`. |
| X3 | Low | Every decrypted entry, and every entry being sealed, was its own page-rounded `SecureBytes`, all held at once: 200,000 small entries (which the parser accepts) are about 3.2 GiB with 16 KiB pages, so an honest large archive could not be restored on a phone. Decoding also copied each entry a byte at a time through the buffer's lock. | **Fixed.** `AccountArchive.open(passphrase:secretKey:each:)` hands over one plaintext at a time and zeroises it when the caller returns; `seal(entryCount:payload:…)` asks for one at a time and zeroises it once sealed. The session decodes each item from one protected copy. The array forms remain, internal, for tests. Test: `oneEntryAtATime`. |
| X4 | Medium | A restore treated every trashed archived item as the person's trash. Items sync had left there (kept after a deletion that had not seen them, trashed without seeing every edit, over a concurrent restore) were skipped as `trashedAtSource` by default, and with `includesTrashed` were trashed again through `trash(id:)`, which made them the person's: the 30-day purge then erased them, a discard nobody chose (F7.7). | **Fixed.** Only `Item.isTrashedByPerson` is trash on restore; anything else comes back live with `restoredFromSyncTrash`. Tests: `syncTrash`, `keptAfterDeletionSurvivesPurge` (restore, 31 days, purge: the item is still there). |
| X5 | Low | A restore kept only conflicting *field* values; another title, another set of scopes and other tags were counted in `unresolvedValuesOmitted` and lost, on the one path meant to keep everything. | **Fixed.** They come back as fields labelled "(unresolved value)": a title and tags as text, a host as a `url` field without `websiteType`, so it is never a scope or an exported website. Flags (archived, trash, exclusion, field order) still settle to the value shown and are counted; a passkey has no text form. Test: `unresolvedAttributes`. |
| X6 | Low | The derivation's NFKD vector used an input NFKD does not change, so dropping the normalisation, or using NFC, passed every test; an archive sealed from one keyboard's spelling of a passphrase could then fail to open from another's. | **Fixed.** The vector is derived from a precomposed "é" and the U+FB01 ligature (Python `unicodedata.normalize("NFKD", …)` of it is the old input, so the expected key is unchanged), and the decomposed spelling must give the same key. Test: `knownAnswer`. |
| X7 | Low | A `discardImport()` (or a newer preview) that ran while a preview awaited the store was overridden: the earlier preview staged its drafts afterwards, so the person's cancel was lost and every secret in the file stayed resident, committable, until the next preview or lock. | **Fixed.** Each preview and `discardImport()` advances `importGeneration`; a preview whose generation is stale drops its drafts and throws `noPendingImport`. Tests: `discardDuringPreview`, `newerPreviewWins`. |
| X8 | Low | Follow-up to X1, from the integration of the four streams: the rest of the session's interchange work still ran on its actor, so `lock()`, auto-lock and sync waited behind it with every key resident: a plaintext file's parse (bounded, but a 100 MB file takes seconds), `AccountArchive.parse` (up to 256 MiB, hashed), the assembly of an archive's entries, and an export's account-password derivation and the new archive's derivation. X1's test also held a stand-in passed to `awayFromSession` directly, so moving the archive derivation back onto the actor would have passed it. | **Fixed.** Each step runs off the actor through `awayFromSession`, named by `AwayWork`, on copied inputs; a parse's IDs come from a `ForkedGenerator` keyed from the session's generator (a copy of a seeded generator would replay the session's own IDs), and an archive's key material still from the session's generator, on the actor, with only the derivation away (`AccountArchive.seal(…wrappingKey:salt:…)`). Tests, each holding its step in the session's `awayWorkHook` and locking (with the step on the actor, every one fails): `lockWinsDuringImportParse`, `lockWinsDuringArchivePreview` (parse and derivation), `lockWinsDuringExport` (the plaintext export's password check, the archive export's password check and derivation); `preDerivedWrappingKey`; "Forked generator" (`WatchwordCryptoTests`, a known answer from Python's `hmac`). |

## N. Review of synced breach-check results (the `scanStatus` record)

Review date: 2026-11-16. Written and reproduced in a remote Linux session against swift-crypto (ADR 0006); nothing
below has run on a Mac yet. The review read the commit that syncs breach-check results (`EnvelopeKind.scanStatus`,
`ScanBinding`, `SyncedScanRecord`, `PasswordBreachEvidence`, `HygieneKeys`, `VaultSession+HygieneSync.swift`, the
ledger's new keys, the write judge's and rotation's handling of the kind; ADR 0005, amendment of 2026-11-16;
`02-architecture.md` §13.9) against the owner's rule (a synced "clear" only postpones this device's next check, and
never hides a breach this device has seen) and the adversaries of architecture §2: **A1** (the store: drops, reorders,
replays, withholds; forges nothing), **A4** (a current member, who can read and change the password), **A5** (a
former member) and **A8** (whoever can write this device's files while it is locked). N1 to N3 were each reproduced by
a test that fails without the fix, and the test stays as the regression.

| ID | Severity | Finding and threat | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| N1 | Medium | An item that left the engine's candidates while still existing (trashed, or saved without a password) was *forgotten* in the ledger: its own results, the breaches it had taken in and its deferral mark all went, and an unlock pruned them for every item without a password. Before results were synced that cost one check (the item came back due). Now it hid a breach: A4 trashes and restores the item, or takes the password field out and puts it back, lets the victim's device sync each step, then syncs a fresh "not found" for the same password; the victim's device has nothing of its own left, shows the breached password as clear on the member's word, and defers its own check with the limit started afresh, repeatable for ever without the password ever changing. An honest household hit it too: a device that found a password breached after another device's older "clear" lost its finding at a trash and restore and showed "clear" until the other device's next pass. | **Fixed.** `ScanSchedule.setAside` (failures only) for a trashed or passwordless item; `forget` only for a permanent deletion (`HygieneEngine.remove`); an unlock prunes only items not listed at all; reports are filtered by the passwords held only while the item holds some. Tests: `removalCannotEraseAnOwnBreach` (adversarial, two accounts sharing a vault, both trash and password removal; all four expectations failed before), `ownBreachSurvivesRemoval`, `deferralLimitSurvivesRemoval` (before the fix the device never checked in sixty days of daily "not found" with a removal every ten). "Hygiene engine" removal test updated: a trashed item's entry stays, a deleted one's goes. |
| N2 | Low | `ScanSchedule.syncDeferral` returned "deferring since now" for an item it had no room to mark (200,000 marks), so the deferral limit never ran out for it. Reaching it needs that many items deferring at once (a member creating them in a shared vault). | **Fixed.** No room, no deferral: the device checks the item itself. Test: `deferralWithoutRoom`. |
| N3 | Low | The ledger's binding key was `HMAC(reuse key, "WW-ledger-binding-key-v1")`, which is exactly the reuse token (`HMAC(reuse key, UTF-8 of password)`) of a password spelt like the label: the two derivations from one key were not separated, against the code's own claim. Tokens are memory-only, so nothing leaked, but a binding key must not be any password's token. `HygieneKeys` itself is separated (its own label and salt; nothing else derives from a vault key, which is otherwise only an AES-GCM key). | **Fixed.** The label starts with `0xFF`, a byte no UTF-8 text holds. Changes every ledger binding; no ledger with bindings has been released. Test: `ledgerBindingKeyIsNoReuseToken`. |
| N4 | Low | The ledger keeps one check per item, so a breach this device saw of a password the item no longer holds is forgotten once this device checks the new one. A4 who changes the password from P to Q (this device checks Q) and back to P, and reports P clear, has P shown clear here for up to the deferral limit, and can repeat it each limit with two more edits. | **Accepted, for the owner.** Every step is an edit in the item's history by someone who could set any password; closing it needs a per-password memory of breaches seen (for example own findings carried into `ScanLedger.reported` when a check replaces them, with their own bound), which is a design choice about how long a former password's breach is remembered. Recorded in §13.9. |
| N5 | Low | Scan records without an item. A check that races the item's permanent deletion writes an observation above the tombstone, which the merger keeps (`settled(afterErasureAt:)`): a record whose item is gone, synced to every device, sealed again at every rotation, never collected (nothing deletes it but the item's deletion, which has happened). A4 can also write scan records under any record ID; unlike junk items, the person cannot see or delete them. | **Accepted, tracked.** Inert (evidence is matched only for listed items) and bounded only by writes, as items are; a collection rule (erase a scan record whose item has a tombstone, or that no item names after a reconcile) is open. A planted record under an item ID of another vault is ignored when read (`scanStatusRow` checks the vault) but makes this device's own publish for that item fail its `.absent` expectation: deduplication lost for that item, its own checks unaffected. |
| N6 | Low | L3 again: an older sealed ledger put back by A8 now also drops deferral marks and reported breaches taken in since. A dropped mark starts the deferral limit again, so A8 together with A4 reporting "not found" can keep postponing this device's own check; a reported breach is taken in again from the record if it is still there. | **Accepted, documented** (§13.8). L3's reasons stand (no counter that a locked background scan could advance); it needs A8 and A4 together, and this device's own findings in the rolled-back ledger stay. |
| N7 | Low | Forward compatibility: a client that predates the kind refuses every segment and snapshot carrying a `scanStatus` entry (`SyncFormatError.unsupportedRecordKind`), and, merging each stream strictly in sequence, waits at that segment for good: once an upgraded device runs one check, an older device of the account (or member of the vault) merges nothing more from that device's stream, items included, and never acknowledges it, so collection stalls too. | **Fixed** (commit 0d04805, ADR 0009). Every kind is critical or ignorable by its raw value (10 to 127 ignorable; `scanStatus` is), so the authenticated kind byte is its criticality. An ignorable kind a device does not carry is kept aside byte for byte (capped at 4,096 entries and 4 MiB per vault; past that dropped, recorded, and replayed from the log after an update), the stream merges on and is acknowledged, and an update processes what was kept without downloading it again; a device holding such entries writes no snapshot, so collection never loses them for others. A critical kind stops the stream at that object, skips nothing, keeps it staged, and reports `SyncReport.requiresNewerVersion`. Tests: "Record kinds a device does not know" (`ignorableKindIsKeptAndProcessedAfterUpgrade`, `ignorableKindFromSnapshot`, `noSnapshotFromAStash`, `criticalKindStopsTheStream`, `criticalKindInASnapshot`, `overflowReplaysOnUpgrade`), "Unknown record kinds in the payload", "Record kind criticality"; adversarial, in "sync log": `kindFlip`, `itemUnderIgnorableLabel`, `flood`. |
| N8 | High | (Review of the N7 fix.) Stashed entries and overflow were never filtered by revocation. An insider publishes one entry of a kind no version defines (raw 99) and is revoked: every device keeps it, so none writes a snapshot again, collection stops for good, and the snapshot that hands on the revoked device's earlier work (§7.8) is never written, so a device joining later lacks that work (reproduced: 40 syncs, no snapshot, the newcomer without the item). Separately, a still-trusted insider could do the same for as long as it was trusted, and held back even that hand-on snapshot. | **Fixed.** (commit bafe195) Each kept entry records the device whose object carried it, and overflow is per author; what a revoked device carried is forgotten except its own versions, which hold nothing back. Per-author caps (2,048 entries, 2 MiB). The snapshot owed for a revoked device's work is written whatever the stash holds, covering only revoked streams. ADR 0009's "no new capability" corrected: a trusted insider can stop ordinary snapshots and collection (no data lost; `snapshotWithheldBy` names it; revoking it ends it). Tests: `revokedAuthorsStashDoesNotStopSnapshots`, `handOnDespiteStash` (both failed before), in "sync log". |
| N9 | Medium | The whole stash (about 5.5 MB of JSON when full) was rewritten with the state after every merged segment, and keeping an entry scanned the stash: a flooder could force gigabytes of writes and quadratic work during a catch-up. | **Fixed.** (commit bafe195) The stash has a row of its own (`SyncStash`, binary; `SyncStateStore.stash`/`saveStash`; a `stash` table, SQLite schema 2), written once per sync; `SyncState.stashUnsaved`, saved with each segment, makes the sync after a crash replay the log. Keeping is a lookup per entry, with counts kept incrementally. Tests: `flood` (one stash write for the pass), `unsavedStashIsRebuilt`, "Sync stash encoding", the state store contract's stash row. The SQLite table and migration have not run on a Mac yet. |
| N10 | Low | The stash's decoder accepted critical kinds and unbounded fields; A8 could plant a kept *item* deletion that `processStashed` applied. | **Fixed.** (commit bafe195) The parser accepts only ignorable kinds, positive versions, keyless deletions, non-empty bounded envelopes and erasures, with counts checked before allocating; the engine holds a row to its caps and replays the log for one it refuses; `processStashed` drops any critical kind unread. Tests: `plantedStash` (adversarial, A8), `plantedRows`. |
| N11 | Low | A reconciling sync threw the stash away, including the revoked device's own entries, which no replay can bring back. | **Fixed** (commit bafe195) with N8: those are kept across a replay. Test: `revokedAuthorsOwnEntriesSurviveReconcile`. |
| N12 | Low | An entry from a snapshot was kept again although the stash held a later version by the same writer, from that writer's own stream. | **Fixed.** (commit bafe195) Skipped as contained. Test: `supersededSnapshotEntryIsNotKept`. |
| N13 | Low | Notes: (a) kept versions do not raise the U5 high water; (b) a session's two engine runs lost the first run's `requiresNewerVersion` and `replayNeeded`; (c) a critical segment passed under snapshot coverage still asked for a newer version; (d) a long mixed-operator `guard` in `validate` risked the Swift 6.1 type checker. | (a) **Kept, documented** (ADR 0009, "The U5 ceiling"): counting unread versions would let any writer raise the ceiling without bound; kept entries are processed in ascending order. (b), (c), (d) **Fixed** (commit bafe195; tests `flagsFromEitherRun`, `coveredCriticalSegmentIsNoStop`; `headerNames` in simple steps). |
| N14 | High | (Second review of the N7 fix.) A planted stash row could erase records and pass the judge: A8 writes a row holding a kind-10 deletion (no key, an erasure summary, a version within the high water); the next sync applied it, remembered the erasure, and this device's next snapshot published it as a tombstone signed with the account key, wiping the result across the household. Naming a revoked device as its carrier routed it past a shared vault's judge. ADR 0009's "advisory only, not worth signing" did not hold. | **Fixed.** `AuthenticatedSyncState` signs each row with the account key under a new `SignatureDomain.syncStash` (raw value 9; a magic inside `pendingDeletion`'s domain would not do, as its messages start with a vault ID and carry no magic), over `"WWST" ‖ vault ID ‖ device ID ‖ stash`, and verifies it (the Keychain's account key while locked); a row that does not verify is refused and the log replayed. Test: `plantedStashRow` (real keys, "sync with real keys"; unsigned, garbage-signed and another vault's genuine row; failed before). |
| N15 | Medium | Any held entry with an equal or higher version in a slot kept out a snapshot's entry, whoever carried it: an insider attributing version 2^40 to an honest device's record suppressed the genuine version, and once the insider was revoked `forgetRetired` dropped its entry and neither survived. | **Fixed.** Only a version held on the writer's own word, or this carrier's, keeps one out. Test: `foreignAttributionSuppressesNothing` (failed before). |
| N16 | Medium | Parsing a hostile row was quadratic (an insertion scan per entry): about 46 s for 8,192 entries in one slot, some 48 minutes at the parser's ceiling, and the engine's caps were checked only after parsing; a suspended iOS app might never finish. | **Fixed.** The caps go into `SyncStash.parse` and the declared count is checked before any entry is read, the bytes as they are read; entries are gathered per slot and each slot sorted once; inserting is a binary search; UUIDs compare as bytes. Test: `hostileRowCost` (8,192 in one slot well under the bound; over-cap rows refused from the header). |
| N17 | Low | Two insiders at 2,048 entries each filled the 4,096 cap; an honest device's entries then went to `overflow[H]`, which named H in `snapshotWithheldBy` and outlived the insiders' revocation. | **Fixed.** A drop past the vault's cap is `SyncStash.crowdedOut`, held against nobody; while anything is crowded out, revoking a carrier frees its entries, its own versions included, and the log is replayed to fill the room. Test: `crowdedOutIsNotBlamed` (failed before). |
| N18 | Low | `forgetRetired` drops unknown-kind entries a revoked device's snapshot carried for honest devices. | **Accepted, documented** (ADR 0009, "Revoked devices"). Keeping them would trust the revoked device's attribution; the honest writer's own stream carries them, or, collected, the later snapshots of devices that read the kind, which every device merges, so the device may lack such a version only until one of those arrives. |
| N19 | Low | A8 deletes the stash row or clears `stashUnsaved`, and the device writes ordinary snapshots again without what it had kept. | **Fixed as far as practical.** Each row carries a generation the state records (`SyncState.stashGeneration`); a row of another generation, or none while the state names one, forces a replay. Test: `staleOrMissingRowIsRebuilt` (failed before). Remained, and was wider than written here: `SyncState` was not authenticated, which reached items on every device (Z20, since fixed: the row is signed). |
| N20 | Info | `+ [entry.isDeletion ? 1 : 0]`-style concatenation (slow to type-check on Swift 6.1); no v1→v2 SQLite migration test; what an older app does with a version-2 `sync.sqlite`. | **Fixed / documented.** `append` throughout `SyncStash`; `migratesFromVersion1` (Mac-only, not yet run); an older app throws `unsupportedSchemaVersion`, deletes nothing and syncs nothing until updated; downgrade is not supported (ADR 0009, docs/02 §7.6). |

Checked and found sound: replay of a record across items, vaults or versions fails at the context (record, vault,
kind, key, version from the row) and again at the plaintext's own identity; a binding copied to another item or
password matches nothing (the item ID is in the message, and the key is per vault and key ID); a record is readable by
nobody without the vault key, and A1 sees only that an item has a scan record, how many observations and results it
holds, and when it changes (which items have passwords, and when devices check: metadata, accepted); the join is
commutative, associative and idempotent with the 32-device bound (the bound keeps the top of one total order, so it
commutes with the per-device maximum), counts and lengths are checked before use, revisions cannot exceed the
record's version, and overflow throws; eviction by A4's many devices costs other devices the deduplication, never a
finding, since this device's own results come only from its ledger; synced times are ignored beyond the tolerance and
clamped to now, and an A1 replay of an old record is superseded per device or too old to count; every await in the new
session code is followed by an epoch check before keys are used, keys stay in `SecureBytes`, and nothing copies one
out; deletions of scan records go through the deletion-recording store; the write judge attributes each observation to
its device and revision, so one forged in another device's name or written after a removal is held aside; a read-only
member writes none; rotation re-seals scan records with every observation's own device, revision and key.

## O. Review of the late-writes change (every part names its writer, S44), and S45 and S46

Review date: 2026-11-16, in a remote Linux session against swift-crypto (ADR 0006); nothing here has run on a Mac.
An independent adversarial review of `git diff 90cd14a ws/late-writes` (c69d97b, 82c78aa: `NoteLine.removedBy`,
`ItemHistoryEntry.writtenBy`, `ItemDiscard`, `ItemMerge.attributedValues` over them, `SharedStreamIndex` format 3 and
`knowledgeCounts`), made while merging it with synced breach-check results (§N), with the adversaries of architecture
§2: **A1** (the store: drops, reorders, replays and withholds, forges no signature), **A4** (a current member) and
**A5** (a removed or demoted member whose late writes reach others through a device that does not yet know). S45 and
S46 (fourth round, §I) were reproduced through the session and fixed here; their rows above say how. S47 was
reproduced by a test that fails without the fix, which stays as the regression.

| ID | Severity | Finding and threat | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| S47 | Low | The merge with §N: a shared vault's take-out rebuilds an item from the versions that count, at a lower version or at the same version by another device, and the session remembered only the version of each item it had shown the hygiene engine, which refuses a copy no newer than one it has seen. A5's late version, merged by an unaware member's device, stayed in that device's hygiene report and was the password it checked until the next unlock (or an edit above it), while the vault's own password went unchecked. Between the take-out and its replay the item is missing from the store, and a reconcile then (a report asked for during the sync, or a replay put off) forgot its breaches as for a permanent deletion, N1 again. | **Fixed** (2176004). The session remembers the row's device too, and a row that replaces the one shown without being newer goes to `HygieneEngine.replace`, which forgets the version seen and keeps the ledger; items taken out are kept from removal until the replay that rebuilds them has run. A replay does not run hygiene side effects again: the engine is reconciled with the store after it, by row identity. Test: `hygieneAfterTakeOut`. |
| S48 | Low | What S46's fix leaves. A claim about a current member's edits can be waited for but never refuted, because nothing says when it was made: a version whose claim was false when it was written is admitted once the claimed device's stream has grown that far, and then supersedes the edits made in between. That is safe while every device applies the check before it merges anything (an honest device's edits then carry only claims that had come true). A device that cannot read the claimed device's stream from its first segment (collected before the vault was shared, or sealed under a key it was never given) believes the claim, so a store (A1) that hides a current member's whole stream from one member's device, with A5's late version, can still have that device launder a false claim to everyone. | **Tracked.** The bound that refutes a claim is what its writer could have seen when it wrote: the acknowledgement vectors of the segments (`SyncPayload.vector`) filed in the stream index, and their causal closure through segments that count. That is a format change of the index and the `WriteJudge.record` call, left for a later round. |
| S49 | Low | False positives of S43 and S44, by design. Everything an unaware device wrote on an item after merging a late version of it is held aside on every device, its own included, once the removal is known: not only edits of the late value's group but of any group, a deliberate removal of a note line, a resolution, a new password. Each is kept in the quarantine (`SyncReport.heldAside`, `quarantinedVersions()`), nothing is lost, but the item is shown as it was before: a changed password goes back to the old one, a removed line and discarded values come back, and AutoFill fills the old password. S45's fix adds a deletion built on a late write: the item comes back, and the person deletes it again. Needs a late write merged before the removal is known (A5 with A1, or an honest race between a removed member's offline device and the record). | **Accepted, for the owner.** It is the direction that loses nothing (§8.7, S28). The way out is for the device to replay its own held-aside edits on the rebuilt item, as new edits by itself (it knows which values are its own), so that only what the late write contributed stays aside; that needs a design for edits whose meaning depended on the late value (a resolution of a conflict it took part in). Until then the UI must show held-aside versions. |
| S50 | Low | Forced replays. A version holding a value, or now a clock entry (S44, S46), that names a listed device whose stream has not carried it waits, and every new segment of an awaited device asks for a full replay of the vault's log. One planted version (A4, or A5 before its removal is known) that claims a device's edits it will not publish soon, or a late version of A5 claiming its own device's edits beyond any carrier, makes every device replay the whole log once per sync for as long as that device keeps writing (A5 writes as often as it likes). The cost is one replay per sync, linear in the log, not the quadratic work of the registry flood fixed in d0b6daa; nothing is lost and nothing diverges. | **Tracked.** A waiting version could be judged again alone when its awaited stream grows, rather than by a replay of everything; and waits on a device of a former member could be refused once its cut is whole. |
| S51 | Low | Growth. `Item.discards` was one vector per group and is now a set per group that only grows, one element per resolution (a revision and a vector as wide as the devices that wrote the item); `NoteLine.removedBy` grows with concurrent removals and history entries with the writes that recorded them. Nothing collects them (they must stay to be attributed). Between honest devices they grow with resolutions and races, which are rare. A4 can fill any of them, attributed to their own revisions, up to the record size, after which a join with that item cannot be sealed and is quarantined (Y37); `StoredItemSize` counts one writer per history entry and no discards, which holds for the first save of an import, the only place it is used. | **Accepted.** Bounded by the payload limit; an insider can already make an item unmergeable that way (Y37). |
| S52 | Low | A scan-status tombstone is not judged: the session deletes an item's `scanStatus` record beside the item, and the record's erasure is by stamp version alone (`SyncedScanRecord.settled(afterErasureAt:)`). When S45's check holds an item's tombstone aside, the scan record's deletion still applies, so the item comes back without its synced results (each device checks it once more, or keeps what its own ledger holds); and a deleting device whose row version a late write raised erases observations written below that version. | **Accepted.** Results are data any device can produce again by checking, and this device's own findings are in its sealed ledger, out of reach (§13.9). |

What the review did not find, having looked. **Copied or forged writer stamps**: a removal, discard or history entry
is an attributed value judged by its bytes (S36), so one that names an honest writer's real revision but was not
carried in that writer's stream with those bytes waits and is never admitted, and one that names the judging device is
refused at once; the attributions are inside the sealed item, but the judge never takes them on their word.
**The old-record default** ("written by the row that carries it"): it attributes an unnamed part to the version's own
writer, so a late writer gains nothing by leaving a name out, and a current member who strips the names from parts
they republish makes them their own, which they could write anyway; a pre-format row from before sharing is the row's
own, as its writer's whole content is. **Convergence of take-out and replay**: the judge is a function of the
version's bytes, the head and the index, so reordering, a cut learnt twice and replays in any order end in the same
admitted set (`LongRunningLateWriteSimulationTests`, which still passes with the wider clause); every replay is
bounded by the log and is asked for only by a new head, a new key or a new segment, so replays end; S50 is their cost.
**Hygiene**: `knowledgeCounts` runs for items only, which is right, since a scan record has no clock and each
observation is judged by its own bytes; the take-out covers scan records; and the engine's state follows the store
(S47), so a replay does not take a synced breach in twice or start a deferral again (the ledger keeps the first mark).

## P. Review of the Apple adapters (transports, HTTP client, device-local files)

Review date: 2026-11-16, of `d80552e..05d42ac` (the adapters of `WatchwordPlatform`), with the adversaries of
architecture §2: **A1** (the store, the network path, the HIBP endpoint) and **A8** (the local-file attacker). Fixed in a
remote Linux session; what is marked *Mac only* compiles only on Apple platforms and has not run yet. The first macOS run
of the adapters (Xcode 27) also found AD9 and AD10, two faults in the tests.

| ID | Severity | Finding and threat | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| AD1 | Medium | `AutoSyncOutcome(_:)` read only the account objects' `transportFailure`, so a vault log throttled by CloudKit (`rateLimited`) was recorded as a success: the backoff cleared and the requested wait was dropped, and the next sync asked again at once. The sharing pass kept failures as strings, so its throttling was never seen; and every vault asked the throttled store again. | **Fixed.** The outcome reads the account, every vault and every shared vault's pass (any throttling: throttled, longest wait; any stop: failed). `SharedVaultSyncReport.transportFailure` is typed and the pass stops at the first. `SyncEngine.sync(transportStopped:)` and `download(transportStopped:)` make no call, merge what is staged and report the failure, and `syncAll` hands the first stopping failure to every later vault. Tests (Linux, failed before the fix): "Automatic sync outcome", "Sync transport failures". |
| AD2 | Medium | The person's "delete data" in iCloud settings purges the zone; CloudKit then answers `zoneNotFound` without the reset flag, and the transport read that as "never created": a listing was empty and the next create recreated the zone and published into it, silently undoing the deletion (against §7.2). | **Fixed.** `SyncStoreMarker` (`InMemorySyncStoreMarker`, `FileSyncStoreMarker`; Linux-tested) remembers on this device that the zone was seen (listed or created); after that every missing zone is `storeReset` and the zone is never recreated, and a marker that cannot be read counts as seen. Only the person's choice to start again may `forget` it (not built). Tests (*Mac only*): "a zone this device has seen and then finds missing is a reset", "a marker that cannot be read counts as seen". Limit: A8 deleting the marker file *and* the person deleting the data costs a re-upload of what was deleted. |
