| AD3 | Medium-Low | The CloudKit listing capped only distinct valid names, so endless cursors of duplicate or invalid records (A1) kept a sync running for ever, and `AutoSyncDriver`'s `inFlight` with it. | **Fixed.** At most `maximumPages` pages, `2 × maximumListedObjects` raw results and `maximumListedObjects` names, past which `listingTooLarge` (new, `stopsSync`). Test (*Mac only*): "endless cursors of duplicate or invalid records end at the caps". |
| AD4 | Medium-Low | One broken object stopped the whole sync: a missing asset (`fileURL` nil, `assetFileNotFound`, `assetNotAvailable`) and a failed coordinated read of one file mapped to `unavailable`, and the engine publishes only when the transport was available, so one record A1 broke kept this device from publishing indefinitely. | **Fixed.** `SyncTransportError.objectUnreadable` (new, not `stopsSync`): that object is refused alone and its stream waits at it. Tests: "one unreadable object is refused alone; the device still publishes" (adversarial "sync log", Linux), "one record with a missing asset is refused alone" (*Mac only*). |
| AD5 | Low | `NSFileCoordinator` had no timeout or cancellation, so a writer holding coordination (the iCloud daemon, another app, a hostile presenter) held a sync open; and the download check ran outside coordination (a file evicted in between was read anyway). | **Fixed.** Each access is a `CoordinationAttempt` actor that owns its coordinator, gives up at a deadline (30 s, raised to two minutes after AD11) or on cancellation and cancels the coordinator; a fetch that times out is `objectUnreadable`. The check runs again inside the accessor. Test (*Mac only*): "a read not granted coordination before its deadline is given up". |
| AD6 | Low | `conflictingVersions` refuses an iCloud Drive object with conflict versions, which leaves that device's stream waiting at it for good; iCloud may also rename a second create of one name to `N 2.wwe`, which the layout ignores, so that copy is never read. Neither is resolved. | **Accepted, for the owner** (architecture §7.3, implementation plan milestone 3): keeping "the copy that verifies" needs the engine's signature check and is an owner decision. Both cases need a device to reuse a sequence number, which the engine prevents unless its listing lags the store. |
| AD7 | Low | `URLSessionHTTPClient`: `UInt64(totalTimeout * 1e9)` trapped on an infinite or huge limit; URLSession sent its default `User-Agent` when a request named none and the device's languages in `Accept-Language`; system and MDM proxies were not mentioned; the decompression limit was claimed though untested; the timeout tests waited on the wall clock. | **Fixed.** Limits are clamped to 1 ms to 600 s (NaN included); a default `User-Agent: Watchword` and a fixed `Accept-Language: *`; §13.8 notes proxies (a TLS-intercepting one the device trusts sees the prefix) and that the decompression limit is untested through stubs; the deadline is injected, so the stalled and dripping servers are tested with a deadline already passed. Tests (*Mac only*): "time limits are held to their range", the "slow" cases, "a request that names no User-Agent". |
| AD8 | Low | `FileDeviceLocalStore` checked only the last component for links (a link above the store directory redirected it), removed a planted link and then wrote by path (a link planted in between was followed), set 0700 only at creation, and marked backup exclusion per file. | **Fixed.** The store is `components` below a trusted ancestor (the App Group container, links resolved once); every component is checked at every operation and created one at a time. Writes go to an exclusively created temporary file (`withoutOverwriting`), moved into place after removing whatever was under the name, so no write follows a link. 0700 and the directory's backup exclusion (which covers its contents) are set again before every write. Tests (Linux): "a link anywhere on the path below the container is refused", "an existing directory is made owner-only again". |
| AD9 | Low (test) | `FakeCloudKitDatabase` evaluated the `CKQuery`'s predicate, which raises `NSInternalInconsistencyException` ("This predicate has evaluation disabled") on Apple platforms and aborted the adversarial process, hiding every suite after it. | **Fixed.** The fake reads the predicate (`TRUEPREDICATE`, or `name BEGINSWITH <string>`) and records an issue and throws for any other shape, so a changed query cannot pass unnoticed. It stays a copy per test target: shared in `WatchwordTestSupport` it would need `@unchecked Sendable` under `Sources/`, which the unsafe tripwire forbids. |
| AD10 | Low (test) | "files of other names are never records" planted the lowercase spelling of the record's own name, which macOS's case-insensitive APFS treats as the record itself. | **Fixed.** That variant is planted only where a probe shows the volume is case-sensitive; a new test pins that a record has one file name (its canonical `uuidString`) and that on a case-insensitive volume a case variant is that same file. |
| AD11 | Low | The first full macOS adversarial run (Xcode 27, three cores, `swift test --parallel`) failed five of the adapters' tests at the same moment, about 110 s in: two HTTP cases with URLSession's own `-1001` timeout instead of `responseTooLarge`, and the first coordinated call of three iCloud Drive cases with `unavailable`. Both are 30-second wall-clock timers (URLSession's resource timeout, or its 15-second idle timeout, and the coordination deadline), so the likeliest cause is a stretch in which the loaded runner scheduled none of these tests' work (the URLSession read loop and the actors around coordination) rather than five logic faults. The tests also planted "sparse" files of 8 GiB and 4 GiB, which cost real disk traffic on any file system that does not keep them sparse. Not reproduced: it needs a Mac. | **Fixed in the tests, hardened in production.** The adapters' suites are `.serialized`, every test that is not about a timeout uses URLSession timers at their 600-second ceiling and an overall or coordination deadline no test reaches (each deadline keeps a test of its own, with a deadline injected as already passed), the URL-loading stub delivers bodies in 16 KiB pieces so the limit is met while bytes are still arriving, and the oversized files are 128 and 256 MiB, still well over the limits. In production the coordination deadline is two minutes, counted only while coordination has not been granted: a granted access is never given up, however late its task is scheduled. The HTTP client's limits stay at 15 s idle and 30 s overall, since a scan is retried and nothing is lost; a device slow enough to miss them only checks later. The same tests also run in the package-tests job, whose log showed no failure; they were exposed to the same timers there. |

## Q. Review of the account screens (`WatchwordPresentation` and the SwiftUI views)

Review date: 2026-11-16, an independent security review of the screens added for milestones 3, 4, 6 and 7, against the
adversaries of architecture §2 who reach the person through what a screen shows: **A1** (the store and the key
directory), **A4** (a member who may write, or a device someone else enrolled), **A7** (whoever hands over an import
file) and anyone who can send the person a message. Fixed in a remote Linux session; the views and XCUITests are not
yet built on a Mac for these fixes. Findings 1 and 8's confirmation were reproduced by tests that failed before the
fix (`AdversarialSharedVaultTests` "after a relaunch…", "an invitation from a verified owner asks before joining");
the rest were fixed with their tests written alongside.

| ID | Severity | Finding and threat | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Q1 | High | After a relaunch every member showed as verified. `keyCheck` was set only by `ingestMembership`, so a restored vault had none, `SharedVaultStatus` reported nobody as substituted or unverified, and the member list took "in no list" for verified: a member whose keys A1 had swapped lost the "keys changed" warning (D17, SEC3), and the sync keyring's S39 check had nothing to refuse by until the next sync. | **Fixed.** The check is made when a vault is restored at unlock and whenever the status finds it stale; `SharedVaultStatus.verifiedMembers` is the explicit list (`nil` with no check on the current head), and the screen shows "not checked yet" for anyone it does not name (§8.4). `AdversarialSharedVaultTests` "after a relaunch a member whose keys differ…", "Screen models: member trust". |
| Q2 | High | A pasted `WWFP1-…` code hid the typed-number field and the comparison toggle, and one tap pinned the contact as `.scannedCode`. Anyone who can message the person can send a code for an account of their own. The role step also said "Safety numbers match" whatever had happened. | **Fixed.** A pasted code only names the account; a typed number or the person's own comparison is still required, and the pin records which. `.scannedCode` is never recorded until the app can scan with the camera. The role step names the account and says how its keys were confirmed (`docs/05-anti-phishing.md` §3). "Adversarial: account screens". |
| Q3 | Medium | The merge's host switches showed the host as the file spelled it, without its reach or exclusions, and never ran `PhishingDetector`: an A7 file could offer an existing item `аmazon.com` (Cyrillic `а`), identical on screen to the real one. | **Fixed.** `MergeHost`: ASCII (punycode) form, the registrable domain in bold, "and its subdomains" and the exclusions, `PhishingDetector` against the vault's items, a warning naming the site, and a second confirmation to accept a look-alike or another alphabet. Punycode known answers from Python. "Screen models: hosts a merge would add", "Adversarial: account screens". |
| Q4 | Medium | A new item could land in a shared vault that an editing member (A4) had renamed "Personal": the list's filter, the editor and the default destination showed names alone. | **Fixed.** Every list labels vaults by ownership and role (`VaultRow.badge`), the editor names the destination with "everyone in this vault can see it" for a shared one. Renaming was left with any writer, for the owner to confirm; **the owner decided otherwise on 2026-11-17**: only the owner or an administrator renames a shared vault, enforced by every device's judge (§R, K9). The labels stay, because an administrator can still choose any name. |
| Q5 | Low | The export relied on `fileExporter` and `FileWrapper.fileAttributes` for file protection, and the plaintext bytes stayed in an array until the screen went. | **Fixed.** The app writes the file itself with `.completeFileProtectionUnlessOpen` (iOS) into a folder of its own, hands it to the file mover and removes what is left; the bytes are `SecureBytes`, zeroised once saved, on cancel, on close and at lock. macOS has no protection classes (§14.1). Whether the mover keeps the attribute across providers is unverified. |
| Q6 | Low | The models' secrets (the merge's values and what was revealed, the export's bytes, typed passwords) went only when their views were torn down. | **Fixed.** `VaultSession.lockEvents()` and `LockSignal`: every lock, however caused, makes each `LockSensitive` model drop them; the removal form moved into `DevicesModel` for the same reason. Model tests over a real lock, and `ImportExportTests` (a lock with the merge review open, and with the export report open). |
| Q7 | Low | `QuarantinedVersion.id` could repeat for two kept versions at one stamp; every shared-vault version got §8.7's "held aside" explanation, U5's implausible ones included; and a version's title was looked up by record ID in any vault. | **Fixed.** The ID covers the key ID and a digest of the bytes; `QuarantineReason` (derived, since the state store keeps no reason: beyond the jump allowed above what the vault holds is U5's, other shared-vault versions the judge's, the rest Y37's), each with its own explanation; a title only from an item of the same vault. Storing the reason with the entry is left for a state-store format change. |
| Q8 | Low | Device names are chosen by whoever enrolled the device, and the screen appended "(this device)" to the name text, so a name could claim it, or reverse the text around it. Accepting an invitation from an owner pinned earlier joined at once. | **Fixed.** Names are cleaned of control, formatting and separator characters and of any "(this device)"; this device is marked from its ID, apart from the name, and sentences quote names. Accepting always asks before joining. |

## R. Review of conflict copies (owner decision, 2026-11-17; architecture §7.5)

Review date: 2026-11-17, by the author of the change, in a remote Linux session; the Apple adapter is drafted and not
compiled. Adversaries: **A1** (the store: plants, replaces, hides and deletes copies), an **insider device** (holds
every key; a forked device is one in effect), **A4** (a member of a shared vault) and **A8** (the local file: staged
copies, the sync state). Each attack below was tried as a test before or while the code was written; "held" means the
test passed against the first implementation.

| ID | Severity | Attack tried, or finding | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| R1 | Medium | *Finding.* A genuine object of another name planted as a copy passed the fakes' signature check (their checksum does not cover the name), was staged beside the genuine object, failed its context at merge, and so stopped the whole position: one planted file, a stream waiting for good. Production signatures bind the name, so there it failed at download; but the engine should not lean on that. | **Fixed.** Every copy's kind and context are checked against the name at download (they need no key), before its signature; a copy for another name is junk for any keyring. Test: `plantedJunkCopies`. |
| R2 | Medium | *Finding.* With the sync's bound nearly spent, a name's copies were fetched up to what was left of the bound and the name was then remembered as examined in full, so the copies not fetched were never looked at. | **Fixed.** A name is examined whole or not at all: the bound is checked before a name, never inside one. Test: `boundPerSync`. |
| R3 | — | Junk beside a genuine object; junk as the provider's current version with the genuine object as the copy; noise, a truncated object, a replaced signature, a revoked device's segment. | Held: each costs one check, none is staged, the genuine object is applied locked-then-unlocked and with real keys. Tests: `plantedJunkCopies`, `genuineBehindJunk`, `plantedCopiesBesideARealSegment`. |
| R4 | — | A flood: 400 copies of one name, 20 of each of 39 more, to stall a stream or stop publishing. | Held: bounded checks per sync, the stream merged from its current files, the device's edit published in the same sync, the flood worked through and then not read again. Test: `copyFlood`. |
| R5 | — | A forked device, to make devices disagree or lose one copy's edits: two verifying copies read in either order, by a device before and after it merged the position, and by the author. | Held: every device ends with the same items, both writes of one revision kept. Tests: `twoVerifyingForks`, `orderOfCopies`, `lateCopy`, `reallySignedFork`. The limit of `10-sync-correctness.md` §8 (a write that had seen one is taken to have seen the other) is unchanged. |
| R6 | — | A member's fork in a shared vault, to have the administrator's write cut bind one copy and the other members read another. | Refused by design: several verifying copies are never merged where there is a judge, at download, late, or from an edited staging row, and the judge is never given a second digest. Tests: `sharedVaultFork`, `judged`, `judgedStagingEdited`. **Open:** the position then waits until one copy is gone; a rule for which copy a cut binds is not designed. |
| R7 | — | A8 adds a copy to the staging area, or edits the count of copies examined. | Held: staged copies are verified again one by one and a failure refuses the position whole; the count only decides whether to look again. Test: `stagedCopyPlanted`. |
| R8 | Low, accepted | A genuine second copy behind more than 15 planted ones, or swapped in while A1 holds the count steady, is not examined. | Accepted: A1 can delete that copy (Y7). The forked author, which holds both, still hands them on in its snapshot. |
| R9 | Low, accepted | A copy that its own author signed for the right name and that does not open (an insider's garbage, or a key not yet held) stops that author's position for every reader. | Accepted: only the stream's own author can do it, to its own stream, which it can as well stop writing. |
| R10 | Low, open | Copies of the account's own objects and of sharing objects are not examined: a junk current version in front of a genuine header or registry entry hides it. | Open. Those are content-addressed or written once per device and are put back by any device that holds them; A1 can delete them anyway. |
| R11 | High | *Finding* (independent review of sync recovery). `SyncObjectCopies.omitted` lumped "over a bound" with "cannot be read now", and the engine remembered the name as examined at that count, which does not change when the copy becomes readable: the name was never looked at again. On iOS a fresh conflict version is not downloaded, and only the main file was ever asked for. So a device restored from a backup that wrote again under numbers it had published, and whose second copies other devices could not read yet, was never told of its own fork, owed no hand-on, and collected the names once covered and acknowledged; `delete` removed the unread copies with them, and its post-restore edits existed only on that device (F7.6). No adversary needed. A reader given only the lower-coverage copy of a forked snapshot waited at a gap for good the same way. | **Fixed.** `SyncObjectCopies.unread` counts what cannot be read now; a name with an unread copy is not remembered as examined (late, refused or staged) and is examined again at every sync, with `objectUnreadable` as the reason when nothing could be read; `collectGarbage` collects no segment or snapshot of its own whose listed copies it has not examined in full. `FileSyncTransport` counts a failed read as unread; the iCloud Drive adapter asks for a renamed duplicate that is not downloaded and counts it, and counts a conflict version without local contents (`hasLocalContents`) as unread, which it does not ask for (no documented non-blocking call does; assumed, not run on a Mac). Test: `unreadableCopyReadLater` (`InMemorySyncTransport.plantUnreadableCopy`), which fails without it: the author collected the name and the reader never merged the fork. |
| R12 | Low-Medium, tracked | *Finding* (F5). In a shared vault any member, not only the stream's author, can trigger the "several verifying copies" refusal: `signingAccount` accepts any listed account whose registry holds the device ID, and device IDs shared between accounts are a documented limit (S33). A member (A4) who registers a device under a victim's device UUID signs a second object for one of the victim's names and drops it as `N 2.wwe`; every device that has not merged that position waits there for good, and nobody may remove the copy. R6 is worse than stated for the same reason, and a late reader stuck at the position holds aside other members' versions built on the stream's later edits (S46). With the sync's bound spent a shared vault reads the current file at a forked position, so the refusal is not unconditional. | **Tracked**, closed by design elsewhere: the audit-fix pull request binds a shared vault's device stream to one account, after which a copy signed by any other account is junk for that name. Doing it here as well would mean a new `WriteJudge` question per copy (which account signed the stream's earlier positions) for a denial of service that A4 can already cause by other means (a write cut it can withhold, S31). No test yet. |
| R13 | Low | *Finding* (F6). Junk-only copies under names a device waits for are examined again at every sync (nothing is remembered about junk that never verifies), and those names come first, so A1 planting 32 snapshot names with 16 junk copies each spent the whole per-sync bound before any merged name: late copies, a fork of the device's own stream included, were never examined, and R4's claim did not hold there. | **Fixed.** Half of `maximumCopiesPerSync` is kept for copies under names already merged (and whatever the waited-for names left). Test: `junkUnderWantedNamesStarvesNothing`, which fails without it. |
| R14 | Low, tracked | *Finding* (F7). A fork's hand-on snapshot is superseded, and collected, like any snapshot, by coverage; a fork's second copy is not expressible as coverage. Rows the forked device adopted from the other copy are marked `published` and never sent again. If another device writes a covering snapshot before it has seen the hand-on, the author collects the hand-on at its next sync, and that device never receives those rows: they exist on the author alone. | **Tracked.** The fix the review names (only a later snapshot of the author's own supersedes a fork hand-on) needs the sync state to remember which of its snapshots were hand-ons of a fork, a new persisted field and encoding version; and the window needs a covering snapshot written between the hand-on's creation and its being listed elsewhere. To be done with the state's next format change. No test yet. |
| R15 | Low | *Finding* (F8). "Never reuse a snapshot name" held only for the hand-on after a reset: a snapshot owed for a revoked device's work, or for a fork, written after a reset with no new version took `lamport = max version`, the name of one the reset removed, which the emptied store accepts again; a device still holding that name in its state (suspected and unanswered, so not pruned) does not read it. | **Fixed.** Every snapshot of a device's own is named above every one it has written (`highestOwnSnapshot`, the names it remembers, its hand-on). Test: `owedSnapshotNamesAreNotReused` ("Store reset: the engine"), which fails without it. |
| R16 | Low, tracked | *Finding* (F9). Up to 16 copies of up to 64 MiB each are held in memory at once for one name (`fetchCopies` returns them together); before conflict copies the peak was one object. Sixteen planted files under one wanted name cost about 1 GiB resident at every sync, enough for iOS to end the process. | **Tracked.** The fix is a transport port that hands copies over one at a time (or a byte bound per name), a change to every transport, two of them not yet compiled on a Mac. A1 can stop sync anyway (Y7); this only makes the stop a crash. |
| R17 | Low-Medium | *Finding* (independent review). A copy that is never readable is examined again at every sync, forever. (a) An honest iCloud conflict version whose contents never reach the device is re-fetched every sync and its name never collected; shared vaults accumulate them. (b) Nothing is remembered about an unread copy under a merged name (R11, rightly), and each such name cost at least one of the late half of the per-sync bound, in name order, so enough of them sorting first (A1 can arrange it; a long-lived vault can reach it honestly) kept a late fork of this device's own stream from ever being read. (c) Where the author cannot read the other copy either, F1's propagation harm remains: the restored device's edits stay on it. | **(b) fixed** (9727d0d): the engine remembers a name whose copies were unread at the count listed (`SyncState.unreadCopies`, persisted, pruned like `examinedCopies`) and examines it after every other name; it is still examined (it may be readable now), nothing is skipped or forgotten, and its author still does not collect it. Test: `unreadableCopiesStarveNothing`, which fails without it. **(a) narrowed:** the iCloud adapter asks for a non-local conflict version's contents after the copies' read (a detached coordinated read of `version.url`, bounded by the coordination deadline; Apple documents that a coordinated read downloads a version whose `hasLocalContents` is false; Mac only, written and not compiled here, unverified until a Mac run); such a name still costs a little of the bound each sync, after the rest. **(c) tracked:** no device can merge what no device can read; nothing is lost: the restored device keeps its edits, the unread copy is never collected (R11), and once either device can read the fork it is merged and handed on (`handOnOwed`). |
| R18 | Low-Medium | *Finding* (independent review, conditional on legacy placeholders). A renamed duplicate that existed on this device only as iCloud's legacy placeholder (`.N 2.wwe.icloud`) has a space in its stem, so the walk neither listed nor counted it: the name was never in `listing.copies`, `fetchCopies` never ran, the download was never asked for, and the author collected the name with a copy nobody had read. SR24's "closed by code" held only for dataless files, while the adapter lists placeholders (`listsUbiquityPlaceholders`). | **Fixed** (8070b0e) in the portable `FileSyncTransport` (Linux-tested): with `listsUbiquityPlaceholders`, a placeholder whose stem is an exact `duplicatedStem` is counted as that duplicate (once, beside its downloaded file or not), and `fetchCopiesNow` counts a copy present only as a placeholder as unread; the coordinated adapter's `fetchCopies` already asks for a missing duplicate's download through its placeholder. Placeholder names are compared as bytes (`placeholderInner`). A duplicate's placeholder is not deleted with the object: the name is collected only once every copy was read. Tests: `duplicatePlaceholder` and `duplicatePlaceholderAlone`, which fail without it, and `inexactDuplicatePlaceholders`. That iCloud still writes such placeholders on current systems is unverified until a Mac run. |
| R19 | Low | *Finding* (independent review of R17, finding 3). The names behind the rest were taken in name order at every sync, so beyond the late half of the per-sync bound (256 by default) the same names were examined each time and those sorting last never again, even once readable: a fork of this device's own stream that was unread when first seen among them, whose download the adapter asks for only when it is examined, stayed unread. And a name was put behind the rest only at the count it was unread at, so A1 alternating its copy count kept it in front (withholding, Y7). The test's comment claimed more than it showed ("at the latest in the sync after"). | **Fixed** (8e44883). Every name in `unreadCopies` goes behind the rest whatever count is listed, and those names take turns, least recently examined first (`SyncState.unreadCopiesExamined`, a turn above every other held, persisted and pruned with `unreadCopies`; a row from before it counts as examined longest ago), so each is examined again within as many syncs as the names need turns. Test: `unreadNamesTakeTurns` (twelve names at four a sync, a fork under the last of them read once readable; then every such name's count changed and a late fork elsewhere still read first), which fails with either half taken out; the comment of `unreadableCopiesStarveNothing` now claims what it shows. A new readable copy under a name with an unread one waits its turn rather than coming first: bounded, and the price of not letting A1 choose the order. |
| R20 | Low | *Finding* (same review, finding 4). `requestVersionDownloads` started a detached task per name at every sync for the same non-local versions, each waiting up to the coordination deadline (two minutes) per version in turn, with nothing tracking what was already asked for: A1 listing 256 names of 16 versions each piled coordinators up sync after sync, and a slow download could be cancelled at that deadline. | **Fixed** (c75c50e). `PendingDownloads` (portable, an actor shared by every copy of the transport): a version is asked for once while its request waits, at most 16 wait at once, side by side, each bounded by a download deadline of its own (ten minutes) rather than the sync's; what is left out is found again by a later sync. Test: `PendingDownloadsTests` (Linux). The coordinated read itself is Mac only, drafted and not compiled; that it downloads the version is still SR24's assumption. |
| R21 | Low, accepted | *Finding* (same review, finding 5), the other side of R18. A literal `.N 2.wwe.icloud` file synced into the folder by A1 cannot be told from iCloud's own placeholder, so it counts as a copy that is never readable and its author never collects that name. | **Accepted**, with R17 (a): storage, not loss, and nothing A1 cannot do by withholding a real conflict version's contents. Documented on `FileSyncTransport` (c75c50e). |

## S. Review of starting again after a reset (owner decision, 2026-11-17; architecture §7.10)

Review date: 2026-11-17, by the author of the change, in a remote Linux session; the CloudKit half and the SwiftUI
are drafted and not compiled. The work was taken over half-built: the design section and the engine's first signal
had been written and nothing had been run. Adversaries: **A1** (the store: empties it, deletes single objects, says
it was reset when it was not, puts an old copy back), **A8** (the local files: the sync state, a CloudKit marker), a
**revoked device** (still holds the account key), an **insider device**, and the person on another device, who may
have deleted the data on purpose. Each attack was written as a test; "held" means it passed against the first
implementation, "finding" that something had to change.

| ID | Severity | Attack tried, or finding | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| SR1 | High | *Finding (inherited design).* A store that merely lists nothing — an emptied folder, or a CloudKit zone deleted on a device whose marker was lost too — was indistinguishable from a new one at the account level: the next sync of any device put back the account header, the device registry and every wrapped vault key, unasked, and went on to publish new edits. The inherited design left this as a question for the owner. | **Fixed**, conservatively. A store that lists none of the registry entries a device retains, while that device's sync state says it has published or merged a log, is reported as `storeReset` and nothing is published into it. Tests: `nothingUnasked`, `nothingIsPutBackUnasked`, `enrolmentAfterAReset`. |
| SR2 | High | *Finding (inherited code).* The engine's sign of a reset was computed after merging: an object of its own missing *and* something merged that nothing listed stands for. A snapshot another device uploaded first stands for this device's own stream, which hid the first half; a device that alone held part of a third device's stream was then never asked, and that part never reached a new device. | **Fixed.** The sign is taken before anything listed is merged and remembered (`SyncState.storeResetSuspected`) until the store stands for everything the device has merged. Test: `thirdStreamHidden`. |
| SR3 | High | *Finding.* A replay (`sync(reconcile: true)`, or one the engine starts for an incomplete stash, which A8 can provoke with one flag in the state file) forgets what the device published and merged. Against a store that was reset it rebuilt nothing, published every row the device ever wrote, unasked, and lost the knowledge of what to hand on; it did the same when the transport merely could not be listed (CloudKit saying `storeReset`), so a replay asked for at that moment defeated the later upload. | **Fixed.** A replay never begins without a listing or while a reset is suspected (`replayPostponed`); the session does not count a postponed replay as done. Tests: `emptiedStoreAndPlantedReplay`, `replayPostponedOffline`, `nothingUnasked`. |
| SR4 | Medium | *Finding.* A snapshot is named by the highest version it holds. After a second reset with nothing written in between, the second upload had the first's name and other bytes; a device that had merged the first never read the second. | **Fixed.** A hand-on snapshot is named above every number this device has used for one (`SyncState.highestOwnSnapshot`). Tests: `snapshotNamesAreNotReused`, `secondReset`, `repeatedResets`. |
| SR5 | Medium | *Finding (inherited code).* With a stash, the hand-on snapshot covered the streams of devices whose objects had carried no kept entry. A kept entry names its carrier, which for a snapshot is not the device whose segment carried it, so a covered stream could have carried entries the snapshot cannot hold. | **Fixed.** With a stash the snapshot covers the uploader's own stream and revoked streams only. Test: `stashIsKept`. |
| SR6 | Medium | *Finding.* A shared vault whose log is gone raised the question for ever, although nothing can be uploaded for it. And the sharing pass published this device's proposals into a store that was reset, before the person had answered. | **Fixed.** `reuploadRefused` is reported for a whole-log vault whether or not anyone was asked, and such a vault alone does not ask; the sharing pass is skipped at a reset store. Tests: `sharedVaultRefused`, `sharedVaultIsNotUploaded`, `wholeLogVaultNeverHandsOn`. |
| SR7 | — | A1 fakes a reset at will: empties the store; says `storeReset` while holding everything; deletes one device's newest segment or its snapshot; repeats after every upload. | Held: nothing is uploaded unasked; a yes to a claimed reset creates nothing; no name is written twice, no sequence number goes back, no item goes back a version. Tests: `claimedReset`, `deletedOwnObjects`, `repeatedResets` (both suites). |
| SR8 | — | A1 empties the store and puts an older copy of it back, so that the store shows an older vault. | Held: no device applies anything older (the merge is a join), the device that holds more is asked, and after the upload a new device reads the newest values. Tests: `rolledBackStore`, `oldCopyPutBack`. |
| SR9 | — | The quarantine and an unpublished deletion across an upload: a snapshot that swept up refused versions, or dropped a deletion. | Held: kept versions stay kept and reach nobody; the deletion goes out as a tombstone and erases. Tests: `quarantineStays`, `quarantineAndPendingDeletion`, `pendingDeletion`, `mergedErasure`, `deletionSurvives`. |
| SR10 | — | A revoked device answers the reset first; a stale plan is confirmed; the call is made locked, or from a device not registered. | Held: `deviceRevoked`, `reuploadNotConfirmed`, `locked`, `deviceNotRegistered`; nothing is created and the store is not started again. The registry uploaded by a trusted device still revokes. Tests: `revokedDeviceCannotAnswer`, `whoMayUpload`, `staleConfirmation`, `passwordChangeSurvivesAReset`. |
| SR11 | Medium, open | A shared vault is not uploaded again from one member's device, by design (a snapshot has no segment for a write cut to be judged by). Its log does not sync after a reset: new edits are published behind a gap no device can pass, replays are postponed, and what a change of head takes out stays held aside. | **Open, for the owner.** Nothing is lost on any device. A sound rebuild needs every device to keep the signed log, or a membership record that restarts it (§7.10). |
| SR12 | Low, accepted | A1 can make the question appear as often as it likes, on every device, by emptying the store or deleting registry entries (which used to be put back silently). | Accepted: A1 can stop sync anyway (Y7), and declining costs nothing more. |
| SR13 | Low, accepted | A revoked device with a modified client fills an empty store with the registry as it knew it; a brand-new device enrolling from that store alone does not learn of the revocation until a trusted device uploads. | Accepted: the same as a store withholding a revocation (§7.8); no existing device is affected. |
| SR14 | Low, accepted | A device that has never published anything in a vault has no object of its own to miss. If it was offline during the reset, returns to a store another device started again, and alone holds more of a stream whose device is gone, the engine does not ask. | Accepted: nothing is lost (it holds the data), the transport's and the account's signals still ask it if it syncs before anyone uploads, and `reuploadAfterStoreReset` hands on whenever it is called. Asking on missing objects of *other* streams was tried on paper and rejected: a provider that shows a deletion before the snapshot that allowed it would raise the question on honest stores. |
| SR15 | Low, accepted | A device whose sync state was rolled back (A8, a restored backup) *and* whose store was then reset finds no listing to tell it how far its stream had gone: it can publish under sequence numbers, or a snapshot name, that other devices merged under other bytes, which those devices then do not read (they do read its later snapshots, which carry every row). | Accepted, and stated in §7.10. Before a reset the newest segment on the transport closed this; raising the next number from other devices' acknowledgements was considered and rejected, because a false acknowledgement by an insider would then leave a gap that mutes the device. |
| SR16 | Low, open | Nothing tells a second device that the first person declined; each device asks its own person. A vault over the envelope limit (64 MiB) is refused (`tooLarge`) and asked about again at each sync. | Open, for the owner. |
| SR17 | Low, accepted | An insider device writes a snapshot that claims to cover streams whose effects it does not carry. Other devices then see their own streams as stood for, are not asked, and what the snapshot left out does not reach a new device. | Accepted: the same capability as before a reset (§7.5, known limits: a snapshot's author is trusted for the vault). No device drops anything it holds. |
| SR18 | Low, accepted | A8 erases a device's sync state after a reset: the device takes the store for new, publishes the account's objects and the rows it wrote, unasked, from sequence number 1 (SR15's reuse). Or A1 empties the store of everything but one registry entry: the account's other objects are put back as before this change. | Accepted. Neither gives the store anything it did not hold; no vault's contents are uploaded in the second case, and the first needs write access to the device's files. |
| SR19 | Medium-High | *Finding* (independent review). `collectedThrough` (renamed `ownUnlistedThrough` at the merge with PR #23, whose Z149 `collectedThrough` is another field) was raised to `min(listed coverage, lastPublishedSequence)`, so a device whose whole stream another device's snapshot covered, and which had no snapshot of its own, had `collectedThrough == lastPublishedSequence`, and sign 3 was blind after a reset, although an honest store always lists a device's newest segment. That is the normal resting state of every device but the snapshot writer, not only SR14's device that never published. A device whose newest edits a returning device lacked was then never asked; its later segments sat behind the gap on the others; a state from before the field existed did the same at its first listing. SR14 was worse than stated, and SR18's "no vault's contents are uploaded" with it. | **Fixed.** `collectedThrough` never reaches the newest segment a device published (it never collects that one), from a listed snapshot, from a snapshot of its own, or from a first listing, except through its own hand-on after a reset, which stands for every segment. Test: `coveredStreamStillNoticesAReset` ("Store reset: the engine"), which fails without it. |
| SR20 | Medium | *Finding* (F4, the CloudKit adapter, not compiled here). (a) After a key reset, a `deleteZone` answered `zoneNotFound` (the zone really gone) threw, and every retry repeated it: the store could never be started again. (b) The probe was a `TRUEPREDICATE` query, which in production needs a queryable `recordName` index that no other call needs; without it the probe maps to `forbidden`. (c) `deleteZone` acts on the probe's reset flag alone: device B with a stale or repeated flag, after device A has filled the zone again, deletes A's upload. | **(a) and (b) fixed:** a deletion answered `zoneNotFound`, `userDeletedZone` or `unknownItem` is taken as done; the probe is a zone fetch (`CloudKitDatabase.fetchZone`). Tests (Mac only, written and not compiled here): `startingAgainAfterAKeyResetWithTheZoneGone`, and `startingAgainOverAnExistingZone` now asserts no query. **(c) tracked:** CloudKit has no conditional zone deletion, so a fetch just before the delete would only narrow the window; A is asked again and nothing local is lost, and A1 (who can set the flag) can delete the zone anyway. |
| SR21 | Medium | *Finding* (F3). The upload published the account's objects with the emptied-store check off and did not check the lock after `startStoreAgain`: a lock (auto-lock, the person switching away) while CloudKit answered made the rest a locked sync that still created the registry entries and the account header, and every vault was a download. A network failure or a kill after those creates did the same. The zone then existed and the registry was listed, so signs 1 and 2 were gone on every device and only sign 3 (blind before SR19, and blind for SR14's device still) could ask. And `confirmUpload` reported "This device uploaded what it holds" whenever no question was pending, including when every vault had been left locked or waiting for a new password. | **Fixed.** The lock epoch is checked again after `startStoreAgain`; the answer counts only while the vault is unlocked at the epoch it was given in (`reuploadAnswered`, for the account's objects and for handing on); before the store is started each of the account's own vaults that has synced is marked `storeResetSuspected`, which only its hand-on or a store standing for everything it holds clears, so an upload stopped part-way asks again; the screen reports success only if every vault ran. Tests: `lockWhileStartingAgain`, `uploadStoppedPartWay` ("sync with real keys"), each failing without its part. A concurrent `syncAll` during the upload is refused by `syncInFlight` (held from before the plan is checked); no test races one. |
| SR22 | Low, tracked | *Finding* (F10). The sharing pass is skipped only for signs 1 and 2. In the upload sync itself, and at a store another device started again, it publishes proposals, resumes rotations and seals again; sharing calls outside a sync (propose, invite, leave) publish into an emptied folder without any check. These are control objects, not vault contents, but they deepen SR11. | **Tracked** with SR11: a shared vault's log cannot be rebuilt after a reset from one member's device, and what its sharing objects should do there is part of the same owner decision. The objects are signed and content-addressed; nothing of any vault's contents goes out with them. |
| SR23 | Low | *Finding* (adapter assumptions). `removeOtherVersionsOfItem` throwing escaped `delete` as a raw error, which ended the whole account sync from the garbage collection after publishing. | **Fixed.** The iCloud Drive adapter maps it to `objectUnreadable`, and the engine treats a deletion refused for one object as that object's failure: reported, kept, collected at a later sync. Test: `refusedDeletionIsOneObjectsFailure` ("Collecting a device's own objects"), which fails without it. |
| SR24 | —, tracked | The adapter behaviour the review found assumed without evidence. Closed by code: a renamed duplicate not downloaded is now asked for and counted unread rather than read (R11), and one present only as its legacy placeholder is counted too (R18). Still assumed, for a Mac run (implementation plan, milestone 3): an empty coordinated listing (`.immediatelyAvailableMetadataOnly`) on a restored device or one where iCloud Drive was just re-enabled reads as an emptied folder, so sign 2 asks, and a yes on a rolled-back state publishes at reused numbers (SR15's limit); iCloud names a second create exactly `N <2..64>.wwe` and renames only files (a duplicated directory is ignored); one `unresolvedConflictVersionsOfItem` call per listed name inside one coordinated read is affordable; version URLs can be read under coordination of the main URL; `removeOtherVersionsOfItem` works without marking versions resolved; a CloudKit query in a missing zone returns `zoneNotFound` with the reset flag, and after a key reset a zone fetch succeeds while a query of its records answers the reset (SR26); a coordinated read of a conflict version's URL downloads it (R17); a zone can be saved again after `userDeletedZone`. | **Tracked** for the Mac run of the adapters; none can be checked on Linux. |
| SR25 | —, tracked | The review's claims with no test. Now tested: a copy unreadable now and readable later (R11); a reset after a snapshot covered a device's stream (SR19); a lock between `startStoreAgain` and the upload, and an upload stopped part-way (SR21); junk under unmerged names (R13); a `deleteZone` that fails (SR20, Mac only). Still without a test: `NSFileVersion` conflict versions (need a ubiquity container); a concurrent `syncAll` during the upload (refused by `syncInFlight`, by reading); a pending proposal at the reset (SR22); a judge vault with the per-sync bound spent; the hand-on snapshot surviving until read (R14); acknowledgements unchanged by a late copy. | **Tracked** as test debt; none is known to be false but R14, which is tracked above. |
| SR26 | Medium | *Finding* (independent review of SR20). SR20's probe was a zone fetch (`CKDatabase.recordZone(for:)`), which reads the zone's metadata; that is not encrypted, so after a key reset the fetch most likely succeeds while the zone's records answer `zoneNotFound` with `CKErrorUserDidResetEncryptedDataKey` (Apple documents the key on errors from fetching records). `startStoreAgain` then found the zone present and left it; every listing kept answering the reset, so the person's "Upload Again" changed nothing and they were asked again at every sync. The fakes modelled a reset as a failing zone fetch, so the test tested the assumption. | **Fixed** (cdece4b; Mac only, written and not compiled here; how CloudKit answers is unverified until a Mac run against CloudKit). After the zone fetch, `startStoreAgain` reads one record with a listing's own query (`probeQuery`: `name BEGINSWITH "account/"`, results limit 1, so no index a listing does not need, SR20 (b)), and a reset answered for the query or for one record in it is a key reset: the zone is deleted and saved again. Both copies of `FakeCloudKitDatabase` now model a reset as CloudKit documents it (`resetEncryptionKey`: the zone fetches, every record read or write answers the reset, until the zone is deleted). Tests: `startingAgainAfterAKeyReset`, which would fail against the old probe under the new fake, and `startingAgainOverAnExistingZone` (one probe query, `BEGINSWITH`, limit 1). |
| SR27 | Low | *Finding* (independent review), the residue of SR18. `lackingStreams` left revoked devices' streams out. A device that had published nothing in a vault (SR14) and merged only a revoked device's stream hands that work on in a snapshot it owes; when A1 emptied the store but for one registry entry (so sign 2 is silent), its own snapshot was missing but nothing counted as lacking, so it was not suspected, its garbage collection forgot the missing snapshot, and at the next sync `holdsUnsharedRetiredContent` wrote the whole vault, unasked. Found while testing it: a snapshot was forgotten in the very sync that wrote it (the pruning uses the listing taken before), so a store that lost it before the next sync showed no object of the device's own missing at all. | **Fixed** (df90ea1). In a vault that keeps a log by snapshots, what was merged from a revoked device counts as lacking unless a listed snapshot covers it (a shared vault keeps its whole log and is unchanged); and a snapshot written in a sync is remembered from that sync, not dropped until the next listing re-reads it. The device is asked, uploads nothing until the person answers, and its hand-on clears the question. Test: `revokedWorkIsAskedAbout`, which fails without it. |
| SR28 | Low (wording) | *Finding* (independent review). `DevicesModel.confirmUpload` showed its success notice only if no vault's report was `deferredUntilUnlock` or `awaitingNewPassword`. A shared vault whose current key has not arrived is reported as deferred at every sync, and is never part of the upload, so with one such vault a complete upload was never told as one. | **Fixed** (36b8127). `DevicesModel.uploadFinished` asks only after the vaults the confirmed plan uploads (`ReuploadPlan.vaults`: the account's own unshared vaults, those `markUploadUnfinished` marks); an unfinished one of those, a stopped transport or a vault still asked about holds the notice back as before. Test: `successAsksOnlyTheUploadedVaults` ("Screen models: after the sync storage was reset"); its first case is the report the old check refused. |
| SR29 | Low-Medium, conditional on CloudKit | *Finding* (independent review of SR26, finding 1). SR26's probe queried `account/` for the plain `name` field only, while the payload is in `encryptedValues`. If CloudKit reports `CKErrorUserDidResetEncryptedDataKey` only when encrypted fields are read, the query succeeded, `fetch` (which reads every field) still answered the reset, the person was asked, the probe found a readable page and deleted nothing: asked at every sync. An empty page counted as readable too, the fake answered the reset for every query whatever was asked (so the tests checked the assumption, not the code), and neither a reset reported per record nor a probe query failing after the zone fetched was tested. | **Fixed** (a331396). `probeZone` queries each of the layout's top-level prefixes (`account/`, `vault/`, `sharing/`) for one record with the payload in `desiredKeys`, fetches the record it lists whole as `fetch` does, and believes the reset thrown by the query, in one record's result or by the fetch; an empty zone is no evidence either way and is left as it is; any other failure decides nothing. `list` treats a reset reported per record as `storeReset`. The fake models three readings (`FakeCloudKitDatabase.ResetReporting`: every query, only a query asking for encrypted fields, per record) and the key-reset test runs under each. Tests: `startingAgainAfterAKeyReset` (three cases, the second failing against SR26's probe), `probeQueryFailsAfterTheZoneFetched`, `startingAgainOverAnEmptyZone`, `resetPerRecord`. Mac only, drafted and not compiled; which reading CloudKit follows is unverified until a Mac run against CloudKit (SR24). Residual: a record a query's index does not show yet but a fetch by name reaches can still answer the reset while every probe prefix lists nothing; the person is asked again at the next sync and the probe looks again. |
| SR30 | Low | *Finding* (same review, finding 2). Two devices answering "Upload Again" at once after a real key reset: A deleted the zone, saved it and uploaded; B, which had seen the reset a moment before, deleted the zone and with it A's upload. The same if CloudKit set the reset flag spuriously (iCloud Keychain still syncing). Only an explicit flag deleted, but one look was enough. | **Fixed** (a331396). The probe runs again immediately before `deleteZone`, and a zone that reads, is empty or is missing by then is not deleted. Tests: `concurrentStartAgain` (A acts between B's two looks; B deletes nothing and A's upload stands), `spuriousResetFlag` (a reset answered once and contradicted). CloudKit has no conditional zone deletion, so the window between the second look and the deletion remains: narrowed, not closed. Nothing is lost on a device in any case: each holds its vault and the upload is asked again (§7.10). |

## T. Owner decision of 2026-11-17: only the owner or an administrator renames a shared vault

| ID | Decision | Threat and residual | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| K9 | Only a shared vault's owner or an administrator may rename it. Decided against finding Q4's provisional answer, which had left renaming with every member who may write. | A4, a member who may write: calls the vault "Personal" (or anything a person trusts) so that someone's item lands where the member reads it. A rule in the screens would not bind a client that does not ask, so every device's write judge refuses a description that is not an administrator's work. Residual: an administrator can still choose any name, so every list keeps labelling vaults by ownership and role (Q4); S57 and S58 below. | **Changed.** `02-architecture.md` §8.4, §8.7 ("Who may name the vault"), §8.10 ("The vault's description"). `SharedVaultError.renameRefused`, `MembershipLedger.administratorStanding(of:)`, a cut on every record that ends an administrator's role, `VaultMetadataMerger` ordered by the description's own stamp, `VaultRow.mayRename`. |
| K10 | Which of a *former* administrator's names stand: those inside the cut of the record that ended the role (implemented), or none ("administrator now", which admits less). | Both keep a read-write member's rename and a late rename out, on every device. They differ in what a person sees: under the cut a name an administrator chose stays when their role ends, as their items do; under "administrator now" it lapses on every device that day, to the highest-stamped name of an account that still holds the role, is held aside on each of them, and returns if they are promoted again. That undoes a write that was permitted and inside a cut, which §8.7 says counts for good. The cut costs one change to the verifier (a demotion to read-write carries a cut) and leaves S57. | **Implemented as the cut**, on those grounds; recorded for the owner because the other rule is the more restrictive. Moving to "administrator now" later only narrows what counts: `administratorSegmentCounts` and `descriptionMayBeVouched` would stop at `holdsRole`, and the cut on a demotion to read-write would go unread. |

Implemented and reviewed in remote Linux sessions against swift-crypto (ADR 0006); nothing here has run on a Mac, and
the one view change (`VaultsView`: the rename button offered only to an account that may, the reason shown otherwise)
is not built. The change was reviewed twice, by the session that wrote it and by the one that finished it, as **A4**
(a member who may write, and an administrator), **A5** (a former administrator, a former member) and **A1** (the
store: withholds, reorders, replays), through real sessions. S54 to S56 were found that way by the first and S59 by
the second, each reproduced by a test that fails with its fix taken out (checked again by the second session, which
removed each fix in turn).

| ID | Severity | Finding and threat | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| S53 | Medium | The gap the decision closes. A vault's description was judged like any value: it counted if its writer's writes did. So any member who may write renamed the vault on every device, and an administrator demoted to read-write, whose record carried no cut because no permission to write ended, kept renaming it for good. Judging by the role alone, without a cut, would have had every device undo an administrator's own renames the day the role ended; judging by what a description claims binds nobody. | **Fixed.** `administratorStanding(of:)`: every description of the owner or a current administrator; of a former one exactly those inside the cut of the record that ended the role; of anyone else none. `MembershipRules.accountLosingAdministratorRole`: a demotion to read-write now carries a cut (the layout of `WWMR` is unchanged and nothing is released at format 2; §8.7 says what a release would have required). The judge asks it of the segment that carried the description (`administratorSegmentCounts`), and the session refuses its own account first (`renameRefused`). Tests: `forgedRenameByAMember`, `lateRenameOfADemotedAdministrator`, `lateRenameLaunderedThroughAMember`, `renamesByAdministratorsConverge`, `renameWrittenBeforeAPromotion` ("shared vaults through the session"); `administratorStandingFollowsTheOwnersRecord` ("sharing and membership"); "Key grants and rotation". |
| S54 | Medium | Found in review. A name is chosen as well as written. `VaultMetadataMerger` ordered descriptions by the version of the *row*, and any member who may write can seal a row that carries a description on (the merge of two rows at one version does so on every device, and it must stay the description's author's, S37). So a read-write member (A4) sealed the name the vault was created with again, above the current one, and a former administrator (A5) the name they had chosen inside their cut, over the owner's later rename: both genuine to the byte, both admitted, both now the vault's name everywhere. And an administrator who expected to lose the role could label a description with a stamp far above its row, to outrank every later rename from inside the cut. | **Fixed.** The description is a last-writer-wins register on the stamp of the write that chose it (`VaultMetadata.descriptionStamp`), which is part of the bytes the judge checks; a carrier's version decides only between carriers of one description. A row whose `writtenBy` is above its own version does not open. It also ends a wart between an account's own devices: a carrier stored at the same version as a concurrent rename could win over the rename. Tests: `olderNameSealedAgain`, `descriptionDatedAboveItsRow`. |
| S55 | Medium | Found in review. A member who joined after a rotation cannot read the segment that first carried an older name and takes it on the word of the version that carries it on (vouching, S42). Any member's version vouched, so a read-write member (A4) sealed a name of their choosing under the current key, labelled as an administrator's old revision, and every device that had joined since the rotation showed it, while devices holding every key waited for a stream that would never carry it: the rule broken on some devices and a split between them. | **Fixed.** Only a version an administrator wrote vouches for a description (`vouchesForDescriptions`), and only an administrator's device seals the description again after a rotation, so honest devices produce no carrier a newcomer must refuse. The seal's age is judged for the name's author alone, so another member's demotion does not take the name from newcomers. Test: `forgedRenameVouchedToANewcomer`. |
| S56 | Low | Found in review. The former author of the vault's name (A5 as to the role, still a member who holds the current key) published the same description again, byte for byte, in a late segment. For a member who joined after a rotation that late segment was the only carrier they could read, it did not count, and the judge then refused the name although an administrator's version vouched for it: the former administrator could take the vault's name away on every device that joined since the rotation, and on no other. | **Fixed.** A description whose carriers here do not count is still vouched for by an administrator's version, as one carried nowhere here is. Test: `ownNamePublishedAgainLate`. |
| S57 | Low | What vouching leaves, as S42 does for items. A name chosen by an administrator whose role ended *after* the last rotation cannot be checked by a member who joined after that rotation: the carrier was sealed under a key older than the record, so nothing says its sealer had judged the name by the cut (a device that merged a late name before it knew would have sealed the same thing). That member's device shows no name and holds the version aside until the next rotation or rename; every other device shows the name. A carrier that a read-write member's device stored when two rows met at one version is likewise refused there, with the administrator's own row of the same name kept. Fail closed, never a name that should not count. | **Tracked.** Rotate after demoting an administrator (§8.10). The way out is for the record that ends the role to say which description it leaves standing (a digest in the cut, which has room), so that a newcomer can check the one name that matters without the segments. Test: `vouchedNameAcrossDemotions` pins both halves. |
| S58 | Low | What the role leaves. (a) A name written by a member's modified client while read-write counts from the moment that member is made an administrator, on every device: standing is judged now (as S12 for writes), because only the writer says when a write was made, and they could write it again at once. (b) An administrator can date a name up to the version jump the engine allows and can seal another's description again; both are within renaming. (c) `modifiedAt` is not part of the bytes the judge attributes, so a carrier can state another time for a name; nothing reads it. | **Accepted, (a) for the owner.** A promotion that must not bring a name with it would need the promotion record to carry a cut as well (what the account had published before it), which a client that withholds a segment walks around; the owner sees the name at once and renames. |
| S59 | Medium | Found in the second review. Vouching (S42 for items, S55 for names) believes a version sealed under a key introduced at or after a record to have been judged with that record in hand. But a rotation ingests membership while it is planned and carried out, after the sync's take-out, and `resealSharedVault` then sealed again whatever the store held. With the store (A1) showing an administrator's device a demotion only as it turned to rotating, the late rename of the former administrator (A5 as to the role) that the device had merged was sealed again under the new key as the device's own row. A device holding every key took that row out at its next sync, before publishing it. A device whose account had joined after a rotation vouched for its own row, kept it and published it, and every member who joined since a rotation showed the late name, while devices holding every key held it aside: the rule broken on some devices and a split between them. Read, not reproduced: a former member's late item values could travel the same way, though only where the store also keeps their stream from the rotating device (a value whose own late segment the device holds is refused whoever's version carries it; only a description has S56's exception). | **Fixed.** `resealSharedVault` takes out what no longer counts under the current head before it seals anything again, for every kind, and seals nothing while the stream index is being rebuilt; the next sync does both. Test: `lateRenameSealedAgainWhileRotating`, which fails without it (the rotating device and a member invited afterwards both showed the late name). No test builds the item case. |
| S60 | Low | Found in review of the pull request. A read-write member's device skips the vault's description in `resealSharedVault` (only an administrator's device seals it again, S55), but its inventory still counted the description, so `pendingRotation` reported `.resealing(remaining: 1)` until an administrator's re-seal reached it, and for good if none did. Nothing waited on the count; what was wrong was the report. | **Fixed** (`sharedInventory(_:resealableBy:)`: the description is counted only for an account that may name the vault, the rename rule's own test, `mayRenameSharedVault`; the planning device still counts everything). Test: `readWriteMemberResealsWithoutTheDescription`, which fails without it. |
| S61 | High | Found in the independent review of the rule. Whether a row of the description was written by an administrator was decided by the stream index's `firstCarrier`, keyed by record, kind and version: no bytes. A row's device is what the entry that delivered it says, and segments force it to their author but snapshots do not, and `sharedVerify` accepts a snapshot from any member who may write. So a read-write member (A4) sealed a name of their choosing as a row at the version of the owner's rotation re-seal, labelled with the owner's device, and published it in a snapshot: on every device that joined after a rotation, `counts` and `vouchesForDescriptions` both found the owner's segment, the row outranked the genuine one on its stamp, and the vault was renamed there (S55 reopened). A member whose registry lists an administrator's device ID could do the same from a later segment in that stream. | **Fixed** without relying on snapshots being refused in a judged vault (another pull request does that) or on streams being bound to one account. Each segment now files the digest of every description row it carried (`SharedStreamIndex.descriptionCarriers`, index format 4, so an older index is refused and rebuilt by a replay), and a description row counts, and vouches, only through the segment of its device's stream that carried those exact bytes; a row whose version that stream carried with other bytes counts nowhere (`descriptionCarriage`, `descriptionRowCounts`). The second route is closed by the same check, by reading: the later segment's signer is the member. Test: `forgedDescriptionInAMembersSnapshot`, which fails without it. |
| S62 | Medium | Found in the same review. The judge opens a version to judge it, and opening raised the device's write clock (`highestVersionSeen`), while a version held aside is never stored and moves no other device's high water. A read-write member (A4) published a rename at the top of what every engine accepts (U5's ceiling); every device held it aside and then wrote its next version one above it, past the ceiling of every device whose store had not moved: an administrator's next rename, and anyone's next edit, was quarantined there as implausible. The mechanism predated the rule; the rule made it trivially reachable. The engine's own per-sync high water was raised by such a version too, before it was judged. | **Fixed.** The judge's opens leave the clock as they found it (`withoutRaisingWriteClock`, around `attributedValues` and the clock check); the engine raises its high water only for a version it merged. Test: `forgedRenameAtTheCeiling`, which fails without it (the rename and the member's item both quarantined as implausible). |
| S63 | Medium, tracked | Found in the independent review (finding 3). An administrator (A4 with the role) publishes, in their own segment, a description whose `writtenBy` names the owner's device. Devices holding every key wait for the owner's stream to carry those bytes and hold it aside; the owner's device refuses it at once; a member who joined after a rotation takes the administrator's row as an administrator's word (`vouchesForDescriptions`), sees the owner as its author, and shows it. Same head, same objects, two names; it stays vouched after the administrator's demotion wherever the newcomer reads their stream from its first segment. `10-sync-correctness.md` §10's "the only divergence is no name" (S57) is therefore not true for an administrator's word. S61 does not close it: the bytes are the administrator's own segment's. | **Tracked**, for the same design as S57: the record that introduces a key commits to the description it leaves standing (a digest in that record, which has room), so every device checks the one name that matters the same way. No smaller fix was found: the newcomer cannot read the owner's segment, and refusing every carrier that names another writer would take every honest re-sealed name from newcomers (the re-seal keeps `writtenBy`, S37). An administrator can rename the vault openly anyway; what this adds is a split view. No test pins it yet. |
| S64 | Low, tracked | Found in the same review (finding 4). An administrator's rename not yet published (A2), overwritten by a late rename of a former administrator (A5) at a higher stamp that the store (A1) showed A2 before the demotion, is lost when the late name is taken out: the replay finds A2's name in no stream, so every device, A2's included, falls back to the previous name, and nothing of A2's is in the quarantine. A published rename is restored correctly. | **Tracked.** The fixes the review names both cost more than they are worth now: publishing a device's own writes before it merges reorders every vault's sync; keeping the replaced row in the quarantine needs to know it was unpublished, and a replay (frequent in a judged vault) forgets `published`, so every replay would hold this device's own published names aside again as false positives under the wrong reason. The person sees the previous name and renames again; nothing but the name is affected. |
| S65 | Low | Found in the same review (finding 5). `sharedEngineDidRun` stamped `judgedHead` with the head at the end of a run. `removeMember` and `leaveSharedVault` ingest membership outside `syncAll`'s in-flight guard, so an ingest while a replay was suspended moved the head, and the versions admitted earlier in the run were recorded as judged against the new head; the next sync then took nothing out (S59's take-out trusts this marker). | **Fixed.** `sharedEngineOptions` returns the head as the run begins and `sharedEngineDidRun` stores that one, and only if the take-out before the run used the same head (`SharedVault.takenOutAgainst`); otherwise the marker is cleared and the next sync takes out and replays. Test: `replaySettlesTheHeadItBeganWith`, which fails without it. |
| S66 | —, tracked | The review's list of claims in §8.7 and §8.10 that no test would catch if false. Now tested: a description delivered by snapshot (S61), a forged description at the version ceiling (S62), a read-write member's re-seal (S60). Still without a test, each read and believed but not pinned: `administratorStanding` and `descriptionMayBeVouched` while a removal is owed; a demotion displaced by a fork not being owed; an administrator demoted straight to read-only, or leaving by request, through the session (and the leave case in the ledger); an empty cut on a demotion and a fixed-bytes record showing earlier records verify unchanged; the replay asked for by a segment at or below a former administrator's cut (`demotionHeardBeforeTheNamesItBounds` passes without it); the guard in `resealSharedVault` isolated; S59's "nothing sealed while the index is being rebuilt" and its item half; S57's second half (a read-write device's carrier refused by a newcomer); convergence in any order rather than two fixed orders, and `VaultMetadataMerger` on its own (commutativity, carriers, the tie on equal stamps); an administrator's description stamped as another's (S63). | **Tracked** as test debt for the next pass over the rule; none is known to be false. |
| S67 | —, tests | The independent review's finding 6 on this round's tests. `replaySettlesTheHeadItBeganWith` drove hooks rather than a race through `syncAll`, and never reached the branch where the take-out and the run began at different heads (`takenOutAgainst` differs from the run's head); `forgedDescriptionInAMembersSnapshot` covered S61's first route only. | **Tested** (2f8105f). `headMovedBeforeTheReplayThroughSyncAll`: a member's `syncAll(reconcile: true)` is held in another vault's listing (a test transport, `RacingTransport`) after its take-out, `removeMember` ingests a demotion meanwhile (it ingests before it finds it may not remove), and the demoted member's late write merged earlier is taken out at the next sync; it fails if the run settles the head it began with without asking `takenOutAgainst`. `forgedDescriptionInALaterSegmentOfTheOwnersStream`: S61's second route, a device the member enrolled under the owner's device ID publishing the next segment of that stream with a description one version above the re-seal; the name stays on the newcomer, the owner and a member, and it fails if `administratorSegmentCounts` admits a segment whose signer never held the role. Still Mac only, untested here: `NSFileVersion.hasLocalContents` and the version download (R17), the download of a duplicate's placeholder through the coordinated adapter (R18), `removeOtherVersionsOfItem` failing (SR23), and the CloudKit probe against CloudKit itself (SR26). Not reached: the head moving *during* the take-out (`unmoved` false), which no transport call separates; held by reading. |
| S68 | —, tests | The independent review's note on S67's test. In `forgedDescriptionInALaterSegmentOfTheOwnersStream` only the owner's and the bystander's checks showed the forged segment had been read and judged (`holdsDescriptionAside`); the newcomer's ("keeps the owner's row") would also pass if the segment were refused for some unrelated reason, a signature or a gap. | **Tested** (a0218a0). The forged segment also carries an ordinary item the member may write; the newcomer and the bystander hold it, so each verified the segment and judged it, and the description was refused by the rule alone. |

What the review tried and did not find a way through. **A rename by a read-write member**: published plainly, above
an administrator's later rename, and labelled as the owner's revision; refused by the owner's device at once (it
knows what it wrote), waited for and never admitted elsewhere, and never shown to a member invited afterwards.
**A late rename by a demoted administrator**, with the store withholding the record from them and from another
member: merged there, held aside on the owner's device, taken out on the member's and on their own once shown the
record, while their items written after the demotion count. **Laundering**: the late name stored again by an unaware
member's device as its own row, in that member's stream, is judged by `writtenBy` against the former administrator's
cut and counts nowhere. **A branch from before the demotion**, of any length, on which the former administrator still
holds the role: it lacks the owner's record and loses on every device in every order (the fork rule, S1), so the cut
that counts is the owner's; an altered cut breaks the record's signature; an administrator who is not the owner
cannot end another's role, with or without a cut. **The honest client as its own check**: a session that renamed
before it heard of its demotion takes its own description out before publishing it, because membership is ingested
before any log is merged. **Convergence**: the judge is a function of the description's bytes, the head and the stream
index, and the merge of the description's own stamp, so arrival order, a cut learnt twice and replays end in the same
name on every device that holds every key; what a newcomer cannot check is S57. **Items**: nothing in the rule reads
an item, and a read-write member's writes count as before (asserted in the same tests).

The second session tried these as well. **The order of arrival, the other way round** (A1 with A5): a device shown
the demotion first and none of the stream it bounds, then the name inside the cut and a late one in one pass; it shows
the last name it can check while the stream is withheld and ends on the name inside the cut with the late one held
aside, as the owner's device does (`demotionHeardBeforeTheNamesItBounds`, which fails with the rule taken out of the
judge). **Taking the name away instead of choosing one** (A4): a read-write member's client publishes a deletion of
the description record; a description is never erased by sync, so the name stays on every other device and an
administrator's next rename lands (`descriptionDeletedByAMember`; a property the rule leans on, which held before it).
**A member's segment in an administrator's stream** (A4, the S33 shape): a member whose registry lists an
administrator's device ID can sign a segment under that ID, carrying a description stamped with it; a segment counts
as an administrator's by the account whose keys signed it, never by the stream it sits in, so it carries no name that
counts on any device, the administrator's own included (by reading `signingAccount` and
`administratorSegmentCounts`; no test builds the second registry). **An unaware
administrator's device as the carrier**, where `lateRenameLaunderedThroughAMember` uses a member's: the description is
judged by the write that chose it whoever's row carries it, and a row sealed under a key older than the record that
ended the author's role vouches for nothing to a newcomer; an administrator's device cannot hold a later key without
that record, because the key's grant follows the chain that contains it. (Holding the record turned out not to be
enough: S59.) **A stamp nobody lists, the vault's current
stamp over other bytes, and a stamp the administrator has not reached yet**: each names bytes no segment of that
device's stream carried, and waits or is refused. **The cut itself**: an empty cut on a demotion counts none of the
account's names, the verifier still refuses a cut where neither a permission nor a role ends, and a record that is
missing a required cut is never signed (`writeCutRequired`). Each test written for a fix was run with that
fix removed and failed: the rule in the judge (`forgedRenameByAMember`, `lateRenameOfADemotedAdministrator`,
`lateRenameLaunderedThroughAMember`, `renameWrittenBeforeAPromotion`, `forgedRenameVouchedToANewcomer`,
`vouchedNameAcrossDemotions`, and `renamingASharedVault` in `WatchwordKitTests`), the description's own stamp and its
bound (`olderNameSealedAgain`, `descriptionDatedAboveItsRow`), the word of an administrator's version over a late
copy (`ownNamePublishedAgainLate`), and the take-out before a re-seal (`lateRenameSealedAgainWhileRotating`, S59:
written first, failing, then fixed).

Seen while reading, not reproduced and not changed here: a rename an administrator made on a device that was
registered again under a new ID before it published the rename (a store restored onto other hardware, W24) is
attributed to the old ID (`carriedOver` keeps `writtenBy`, as S37 requires), whose stream never carried it. By the
judge as written, other members would wait for that stream for good and the device would take the name out at the next
change of head; the person would have to rename again. The attribution is older than this change and applies to any
description of a shared vault, whoever may write one. For a follow-up, with a test.

A trade V50 made (V54). A former administrator's rename inside the cut that ended the role, stamped by one of their
own devices that was revoked afterwards, is believed by a member whose device merged that device's stream before the
revocation (the segment carried it, and the cut admits it, `administratorSegmentCounts`), and refused by a member for
whom that stream never carries (`neverCarriedValueCounts` now asks for the role held now). So a member who joins after
the revocation can show another name than one who was there before it. It fails safe (the name is held aside, never
shown as a name someone without the role chose), and an administrator's next rename ends it on every device; it is
recorded, not changed.

## U. Vault sharing codes (ADR 0010; architecture §8.4)

Implemented and reviewed in a remote Linux session against swift-crypto (ADR 0006), by the session that wrote it, as
**A1** with the sharing board and the key directory (reads, drops, delays, replaces and invents every object, and
answers each device differently), **A4** with a device of their own, and the person on either device. The SwiftUI
screens and the VisionKit scanner are drafted and not built; nothing here has run on a Mac. Every threat in the ADR's
security analysis has a test, listed in `04-test-plan.md` §6.4; "held" means the test passed against the first
implementation, "finding" that something had to change. V17 onwards are the follow-up audit of the sharing surface after the first
